COSC 101 Foundations of Computer Science 
(2 Lecture, 3 Laboratory – 3 Credits) 

Prerequisite: COSC 114

Course Catalog Description: 
This course provides a comprehensive introduction to computer science, designed to give students a broad understanding of the field. The aim is to explore various topics, including number systems, data storage, data operations, and computer organization. It also equips students with a foundation and practical knowledge of operating systems, algorithms, data structures, and the social and ethical issues in computing. In addition, the C language is introduced to the students.

Textbooks: 
Foundations of Computer Science by Behrouz Forouzan, Publisher: Cengage, 5th Edition, 2023. ISBN-10: 1473787327, ISBN-13: 978-1473787322.
C Programming Absolute Beginner's Guide by Dean Miller and Greg Perry, Que Corporation, 3rd Edition, 2013. ISBN-10: ‎0789751984, ISBN-13: ‎978-0789751980.

Course Topics: 
Introduction (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 1)
C programming basics, input/output (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part I, II)
Number systems (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 2)
Data types and operators, control structures (condition) (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part II, III)
Data storage (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 3)
Operations on data (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 4)
Control structures (repetition/loops) (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part III)
Computer organization (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 5)
Data structures (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 12)
Arrays and pointers (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part IV)
Dynamic memory allocation and functions (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part IV, V) 
Recursion; file handling (2 lectures) (Miller and Perry, Part V)
Operating systems (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 7)
Algorithms (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 8)
Social and ethical issues (2 lectures) (Forouzan, Chapter 22)

Structure and learning methodologies: 
Lectures are 2 x 50 minutes; Laboratory sessions are 1 x 150 min per week. The course is delivered through classroom lectures, problem-solving sessions, and laboratory work done individually. Individual assignments and quizzes are held throughout the semester to reinforce knowledge of the material.

Honor Code Academic Pledge:
"As a Khalifa University student, I will not lie, cheat, steal, or use any unfair means in academic work
and will behave according to university rules and UAE societal norms and expectations."
TEACHING PLAN (Lecture):
Teaching Topic(s)
Week
1 Introduction to chemical engineering; dimensions &units, unit conversion; dimensional
homogeneity & dimensionless numbers; data analysis, representation, fitting
2 Process variables: Mass, volume, specific gravity, flow rate, chemical composition, mole
and mass fractions, pressure, temperature
3 Material Balances: basic process units; flowcharts, continuous, batch, semi-batch
processes; basis of calculation; degree of freedom analysis
4 Multi-unit processes. Recycle, bypass
5 Reactive processes: stoichiometry; conversion, extent of reaction; chemical equilibrium;
multiple reactions
6 Atomic species balances; Combustion
7 Single-phase systems: PVT relations; ideal gas; gas mixtures; real gas equations of state;
corresponding states; compressibility factor
8 Multi-phase systems: Phase equilibria; phase diagrams; vapor pressure; the Gibbs phase
rule; single-condensable component VLE; humidity
9 Multi-component VLE; Raoult’s & Henry’s Laws; bubble point & dew point; VLE
phase diagrams; solutions; solubility; liquid miscibility; ternary phase diagrams;
adsorption
10 Energy and energy balances: Forms of energy; 1st law of thermodynamics; energy
balance on closed systems
11 Energy balances on open systems; Enthalpy; steam tables; mechanical energy balance;
Bernoulli equation
12 Nonreactive processes; hypothetical paths for ΔH determination; pressure & temperature
changes; heat capacity & sensible heats
13 Phase changes; latent heats; humidification; psychometric charts; heat of mixing and
solution; enthalpy-concentration charts; combined materials & energy balances
14 Energy balances on reactive processes: Heat of reaction; Hess’s law; heat of formation;
ΔH by heat of reaction & heat of formation approaches
15 Fuels & combustion; adiabatic flame temperature

Syllabus Supplement for Students For Spring 2023
Organizational Details
Course Code and Title: CHEM 115 General Chemistry I
Spring 2023
Semester:
Instructor: Dr. Emilia Oueis
Contact Email: Emilia.oueis@ku.ac.ae
Office Room Number: 2010
Office Hours: Mondays and Wednesdays from 10:00 to 12:00 pm
Assessment Methodology
Tentative Dates Weight
Coursework: Quiz # 1 06 Feb – 10 Feb
Quiz # 2 27 Feb – 03 Mar 20%
Quiz # 3 03 Apr – 07 Apr
Quiz # 4 17 Apr – 21 Apr
Aleks Objectives 10%
Laboratory: Lab Reports and Lab Assignments 15%
Semester Examination: Midterm Exam 06 Mar – 10 Mar 20%
Final Examination: TBC by the Registrar’s Office 35%
Instructor Policy on late Submission of Assignments:
 If the student is absent for a Midterm Exam, the Final Exam or a Laboratory Session, he/she must
apply KU rules and regulations to obtain an approval of the excuse.
 If a valid excuse is provided for a missed Midterm Exam, either the percentage for that exam will
be added to the percentage for the Final Exam or a make-up exam will be scheduled.
 Quiz attendance must be in your own lecture/lab section.
 A mark of zero will be assigned for each missed quiz in either lecture or lab, if a valid excuse is not
provided. Please note that no make-up quizzes will be given.
 Lowest quiz will drop off
For each pre-lab quiz and lab report, failure to submit it by the deadline results in a zero mark. There is
no make-up for pre-lab quizzes. Cheating in any assignment or lab report results in a zero mark.
Honor Code Academic Pledge:
"As a Khalifa University student, I will not lie, cheat, steal, or use any unfair means in academic work
and will behave according to university rules and UAE societal norms and expectations."

The official Khalifa University of Science and Technology grading system uses letter grades with
pluses and minuses. Passing grades range from A to D; F is a fail grade. E ach letter grade is
assigned a grade point on a fo ur - point scale as set out below:

Very Good

F 0.00 Fail
WF 0.00 Withdrew Failing
Additional letter grades are used by the Registrar to denote special cases. These letter grades do
not have corresponding grade points and are not used in calculating a student’s grade point
average.
Letter Grade Description
W Withdrew between end of late registration and deadline for course withdrawal.
Withdrew Passing after the deadline for course withdrawal through the last day
WP
of classes. A WP grade must be approved by the dean (or designee).

AUD Audit

Failure Due to Academic Dishonesty (This grade can only be assigned after an
XF
academic dishonesty hearing. A student may petition to change this grade to F.)

--- Page 1 ---
COSC 201 Computer Systems Organization
(2 Lecture, 3 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: COSC 101
Course Catalog Description:
This course thoroughly explains computer logical organization, including components and
interrelationships. It covers the central processor’s native language and basic architectures for
high-performance design. Topics include Von Neumann architecture, low-level aspects of C
programming, data representation, computer arithmetic, assembly language programming,
and digital logic design.
Textbook:
• Computer Systems: A Programmer’s Perspective by Randal E. Bryant and David R.
O’Hallaron. Publisher: Pearson, 3rd Edition, 2015. ISBN: 978-0134092669.
Reference Materials:
• Low-Level Programming: C, Assembly, and Program Execution on Intel® 64
Architecture by Igor Zhirkov. Publisher: Apress, 1st Edition, 2017. ISBN: 978-
1484224021.
Course Topics:
1. Overview: Bit representation of information, program translation, compilation systems. (2
lectures)
2. Overview: Hardware organization, processors, instructions, memory hierarchies. (2
lectures)
3. Assembly programming: Introduction and program structure. (2 lectures)
4. Low-level C programming: Pointers and arrays, functions, dynamic memory management.
(2 lectures)
5. Representation and manipulation of information: information storage, integer
representations, integer arithmetic, Floating point, arithmetic, and logical operations. (4
lectures)
6. Machine-level representation of programs: Assembly addresses and memory allocation. (4
lectures)
7. Processor architecture: Digital logic design. (4 lectures)
8. Processor architecture: Instruction set architecture, sequential and pipelined
implementations. (4 lectures)
9. Memory hierarchy: Storage technologies, locality, cache levels, I/O, and disks. (4 lectures)
10. Optimizing program performance: Eliminating loop inefficiencies, reducing procedure
calls, eliminating unneeded memory references, loop unrolling, and Enhancing parallelism.
(2 lectures)
Course Structure and Methodology:
Lectures are 2 x 50 minutes; Laboratory sessions are 1 x 150 min per week. The course is
delivered in the form of classroom lectures, problem solving sessions, and laboratories work
done individually. Individual assignments and quizzes are held throughout the semester to
reinforce knowledge of the material.
1

--- Page 2 ---
Lab Topics:
1. Assembly programming: Introduction and program structure.
2. Low-level C programming: Pointers and arrays, functions, dynamic memory
management.
3. Representation and manipulation of information: information storage, integer
representations, integer arithmetic
4. Representation and manipulation of information: Floating point arithmetic
5. Machine-level representation of programs: Assembly addresses and memory, flow
control, stack frames.
6. Machine-level representation of programs: Instructions, array allocation and access.
7. Processor architecture: Instruction set architecture.
8. Processor architecture: sequential and pipelined implementations.
9. Memory hierarchy: Storage technologies, locality, cache levels.
10. Memory hierarchy: writing cache-friendly code, I/O and disks.
11. Optimizing program performance: Eliminating loop inefficiencies, reducing procedure
calls, eliminating unneeded memory references, loop unrolling.
12. Optimizing program performance: Enhancing parallelism, understanding memory
performance, identifying, and eliminating performance bottlenecks.
Computing Resources:
A standard computer science lab with personal computers; C compiler and Assembler; systems
diagnosis and performance monitoring tools.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools:
Coursework (quizzes, homework) 15%
Lab Work 15%
Project 20%
Semester Examination 20%
Final Examination 30%

ENGL 101 Academic English I
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: TOEFL iBT 79, EmSAT 1400, or IELTS 6.0
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces reading, writing and digital composing in scientific and professional fields.
Assignments include writing about numbers analytically and interpretively; abstracting and
summarizing; and citing and referencing evidence. Extended written assignments include a review of
evidence supporting a claim and a focused essay with a supporting digital presentation.
Textbook:
Storytelling with Data: A Data Visualization Guide for Business Professional by Cole Nussbaum
Knaflic, Publisher: Wiley, 1st edition, 2015, ISBN: 9781119002253
Reference Materials:
• IEEE Editorial Style Manual (available online: https://www.ieee.org/content/dam/ieee-org/ieee/
web/org/conferences/style_references_manual.pdf
• University of Pittsburgh Library System: Citation Styles (available online:
https://pitt.libguides.com/citationhelp)
Course Topics:
1. Incorporating data and figures into written explanations and arguments (6 lectures)
2. Writing abstracts and summaries for technical and scientific reports (6 lectures)
3. Using appropriate style guides (IEEE) to cite sources and provide bibliographic references (6 lectures)
4. Reading carefully or "closely" to trace the evidence used to make an argument (5 lectures)
5. Using online databases (e.g., JSTOR; EBSCO) and generating keywords to find appropriate
evidence for research-based reports (6 lectures)
6. Integrating information from multiple sources in written explanations an arguments (8 lectures)
7. Taking notes and paraphrasing to avoid plagiarism (4 lectures)
8. Composing digitally using advanced features of PowerPoint, video editing, and other multimodal
software (4 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 2 x 75-minute sessions per week. Instruction will be a combination of lectures/seminar
discussions, writing/composing workshops, and small-group activities.
Laboratory and/or Computer Resources: None. Students will use their own laptops.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Individual short writing assignments 20%
Group short writing assignment 10%
Individual Writing: Supporting or refuting a claim 30%
Individual Writing: Focused essay 30%
Digital presentation 10%
August 2021 1

• As a general policy and at the discretion of the instructor, students receiving a grade of
less than 70% for any assessment will have the opportunity to revise or retake the
assessment one time.
• Students who are found to plagiarize (copy in any form) text, images, or other materials

opportunity to completely revise their work. A second violation of plagiarism will result
in failure or penalization and a report to the Chair of English, the Dean of the College of
Arts and Sciences, and ultimately the Senior Vice President for Academic and Student
Services. Students are advised to consult the Handbook for all policies related to
plagiarism and other forms of cheating.

GENS 101 Grand Challenges
(2 Lecture, 4 Instructional Studio – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course surveys science and engineering disciplines and highlights interdisciplinary, real-world
applications, especially addressing Grand Challenge problems facing humanity. The course introduces
aspects of problem-solving ranging from the formulation of questions and problems to the testing of trial
solutions within a standard framework. Students discuss ethical, social, economic, environmental, and
technical aspects of Grand Challenges, and they contribute to collaborative and project-based activities.
Textbook: None
Reference Materials:
 Strategies for Creative Problem Solving by H. Fogler, S. LeBlanc, and B. Rizzo. Publisher:
rd
Pearson, 3 edition, 2013, ISBN: 9780133091663.
 Grand Challenges for Science in the 21st Century edited by J.W. Vasbinder, B. Gulyás, and
J.Y.H Sim. Publisher: World Scientific, 2018, ISBN: 9789813276680.
 Grand Challenges for Engineering: Imperatives, Prospects, and Priorities: Summary of a Forum
by Steve Olson. Publisher: National Academies Press, 2016, ISBN: 9780309438964.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to Grand Challenges (3 lectures)
a. Definition, history, and survey of Grand Challenges
b. Ways of responding to Grand Challenges with example problems and solutions
c. Ethical considerations related to Grand Challenges
d. Grand Challenges and KU
2. Teamwork and communication (7 lectures)
a. Team roles, coordination of work, introduction to project management
b. Effective meeting strategies and guidelines for conflict resolution
c. Peer evaluation and effective feedback
d. Effective reports and presentations
3. Information collection and evaluation (3 lectures)
a. Relevance, quality, authoritativeness, and credibility of information
b. From information to ideas
c. Types of information: quantitative versus qualitative information
d. Analysis and interpretation of information
4. Defining a problem (2 lectures)
a. Information gathering to define a problem/question
b. Situational contexts and constraints (physical, social, economic, environmental, and
ethical (provide methodology to apply))
c. Sub-problems and use of representations
5. Developing useful models and solution designs (4 lectures)
a. Identifying and/or developing applicable models
UCC: April 2022 1

--- Page 2 ---
KU101 Grand Challenges (2-4-4)
______________________________________________________________________________
b. Proposing hypotheses
c. Identifying response options, functions/features, specifications
d. Developing conceptual designs and prototypes
e. Choice of tools
6. Testing models and solution designs (11 lectures)
a. Experiment and/or test design
b. Data collection and testing of models, hypotheses, and/or prototypes
c. Analysis of results
d. Iterative testing and correction, refinement or optimization of solutions
Tutorial Topics – students complete two required topics and (at least) four elective topics:
1. Studio tool safety (required) 11. Robotics
2. Studio tool use (required) 12. Chemistry kit use
3. CADD drawing I 13. Biology kit use
4. CADD drawing II 14. Programming
5. 3D printing 15. Rocketry
6. Machining I 16. Remote sensing
7. Machining II 17. Data visualization II
8. Electronics 18. Data and statistics
9. Data visualization I 19. Technical report writing
10. Chemical and biological safety 20. Technical presentations
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are 2 x 50 minutes per week and Studio sessions are 2 x 100 minutes per week. Students
prepare for class sessions by viewing assigned videos, websites, and/ or documents. Students maintain
individual portfolios for reflection on the choice of a major, Grand Challenge issues, and project work.
Students train individually on specific skills via course Tutorials. Teams of 4 to 6 students work
collaboratively on Studio Activities and a Grand Challenge project. Studio Activities expose students to
the entire problem definition, solution development, and testing process. For th eir selected topic, each
team develop s a problem statement, solution proposal, final oral presentation, and written report
(includ ing self and peer evaluations components).
Instructional Studio and Computer Resources:
Course materials including Portfolio assignments, Tutorial and Instructional Studio Activities, and
instructions for the course project, are available via university online platforms. Students need access to
the following software: Google Earth Pro and Tableau for Students. The Instructional Studio includes
the following equipment: Robotics kit s, Adhesive & Epoxy kits, 3D Printer, Electronics k its, Biomedical
kits, Cool Tool machining tools, Chemistry kit s, Biology kit s, Data A cquisition kits, and hand tools.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Homework, portfolio assignments) 20%
Tutorials 15%
Studio Activities 15%
Grand Challenge Milestones 25%
Grand Challenge Solution (report and presentation) 25%

HUMA 140 Introduction to Psychology
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Pre-requisite: ENGL 101 or ENGL 111
Course Catalog Description:
This course examines historical and current topics related to the mind and behavior with research methods
and the scientific method as a foundation. Areas covered in this course include biology, development,
memory, learning, social psychology, personality, psychological disorders, health psychology, and
positive psychology.
Textbook:
th
Psychology in Everyday Life by David G. Myers and C. Nathan Dewall, Publisher: Worth Publishers, 5
Edition, 2019, ISBN: 9781319133726
Reference Materials:
th
Discovering Statistics Using IBM SPSS Statistics by Andy Field, Publisher: SAGE Publications Ltd., 5
edition, 2018, ISBN: 9781526419514
Motivation, Altruism, Personality, and Social Psychology by Michael Babula, Publisher: Palgrave
Macmillan, 2013, ISBN: 9781137031280
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to psychology: contemporary psychology, early research, pioneers (3 lectures)
2. Research methodologies: planning and conducting research, analyzing data, reporting results, and
research ethics (4 lectures)
3. The biology of the mind and consciousness: neuroanatomy and neurophysiology, brain areas, the
endocrine system, and stages of sleep (6 lectures)
4. Development through the lifespan: genes, chromosomes, inheritance of behavior, epigenetics,
continuity and stage theory, and stability and change in behavior (3 lectures)
5. Learning: classical conditioning, operant conditioning, evolutionary influences on behavior, and
observational learning (5 lectures)
6. Memory: the information-processing model; encoding, storage, and retrieval of memories,
forgetting, memory construction errors, and strategies to improve memory (4 lectures)
7. Motivation and emotion: physiology of hunger and motivational concepts such as drives, incentives,
arousal theory, and Maslow’s hierarchy of needs (1 lecture)
8. Stress, health psychology, and positive psychology: stressors, stress reactions, the immune system,
stress management techniques, coping with stress, and positive psychology interventions to improve
overall life satisfaction (6 lectures)
9. Social psychology: social thinking, attitudes versus behavior, conformity, groupthink, aggression,
and altruism (3 lectures)
10. Personality: psychodynamic theories, new advances in neuroscience, biological basis for personality
traits, social-cognitive theories, and self-esteem (4 lectures)
11. Psychological disorders: psychological disorders such as anxiety, substance use disorders, major
depressive and bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, eating disorders, and anti-social personality disorders
(6 lectures)
Syllabus and Supplement 1

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 4 x 110 minutes per week. For the final project, students are to create a poster and give a
10 to 15 minute in-class presentation or podcast.
Computer Resources:
Access to MS Excel and/or IBM SPSS is needed for students who optionally elect to undertake statistical
analysis in psychology for the final project.

Description of the Assessments
Quizzes – (10% each)
Three quizzes worth 10% each are required for this course. Each quiz is 25 questions and will test recall
and recognition of the material studied. The first quiz covers material from introduction to psychology to
development through the lifespan. The second quiz covers material from learning to social psychology.
The third quiz covers material from personality to psychological disorders. Each quiz will consist of 25
questions.
Syllabus and Supplement 2

--- Page 3 ---
HUMA 140 Introduction to Psychology (3-0-3)
___________________________________________________________________________________________
Research Assignment (10%)
Students are required to undertake the Gosling et al. (2003) Ten Item Personality Inventory (TIPI) which
is a short measure for the Costa and McCrae (1992) Five Factor Inventory for personality traits. Students
will discuss their results and use the literature to discuss whether their personality traits align to music,
language, and other personal preferences in social situations. Using the literature, students will be required
to estimate whether the TIPI is valid and reliable. The students will be required provide critical analysis
as to whether there exists a neurobiological basis that explains personality traits. Students are required to
obtain a minimum of three peer reviewed journal citations. All references must be IEEE compliant.
Midterm Exam (30%)
This is a cumulative in-class exam held halfway through the academic semester. The exam will consist
of three essay questions worth 75% as well as 25 fill-in-the-blank and multiple choice questions worth
25%.
Final Project (30%)
Students will provide an in-class poster presentation on a selected topic. Students will devise a research
topic based on the topics studied in the course and seek approval of the research topic form the course
leader. Once selected, the research topic is not subject to change. Students will need to use a section
highlighting the research methods used for the poster. Students need to conduct a professional
presentation about the topic using at least five peer reviewed journal citations. All references must be
IEEE compliant.

Instructor Policy on Assessments:
• The solutions to assigned problems will not be collected and graded. Students are highly
encouraged to spend the time to work out the problems. The primary purpose of the assigned
problems is to develop problem solving skills, think more deeply about the course material, and
prepare for upcoming quizzes and exams. Effort given for studying and doing the problems in
this course is directly related to performance in the quizzes and exams, and as a result in the final
grade.
• If a student believes that there might be an error in grading a quiz or an exam, he/she should see
the instructor within one (1) week after the graded assessment is returned. It should be noted that
the entire exam will be re-graded not just the problem in question, in order to ensure consistence
and fairness. Final course grades may only be contested by following the approved academic
appeal procedures.
• There will be no make-up quizzes. A make-up exam will be allowed under extenuating
circumstance, such as hospitalization or bereavement. Even in these situations, students should
realize that the instructor has the right not to accept an authenticated excuse. The excuse must be
authenticated by Student Success Department and brought to the instructor immediately (within

CHEG 350 Materials Science & Engineering
(3 lectures – 3 credits)
Prerequisites: CHEM 116, PHYS 122
Course Catalog Description:
Introduction to materials science and engineering. Metals, alloys, ceramics, polymers, and
composites; inter-atomic bonding, crystal structure and defects; diffusion, nucleation and
microstructure; phase diagrams and phase transformations; mechanical properties; material
failure; corrosion and degradation.
Textbook:
Materials Science and Engineering: An Introduction by William D. Callister, Publisher: Wiley,
th
9 Intl Edition, 2014, ISBN-13: 9781118319222
Reference Materials:
th
Introduction to Materials Science for Engineers by James F. Shackelford, Prentice Hall, 8 Ed.,
2015.
th
Foundations of Materials Science and Engineering by William F. Smith, McGraw-Hill, 5 Ed.,
2010.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction. Atomic structure and interatomic bonding. (4 lectures)
2. The structure of crystalline solids (4 lectures)
3. Imperfections in solids (3 lectures)
4. Diffusion (3 lectures)
5. Mechanical properties of metals (4 lectures)
6. Dislocations and strengthening mechanisms. (3 lectures)
7. Failure: fracture, fatigue, creep (4 lectures)
8. Phase diagrams (4 lectures)
9. Phase transformations(4 lectures)
10. Ceramic materials (4 lectures)
11. Polymers and Composite materials (4 lectures)
12. Corrosion and electrochemistry. (4 lectures)
Structure and learning methodologies:
The course will be delivered through three 50-min PowerPoint-enhanced lectures per week.
Homework problems/questions will be given regularly to help students elaborate the subjects
taught, and are considered as assigned homework every student should work out and deliver.
On average, one-week time will be available for each homework assignment that the students
should distribute evenly in order to work on and deliver the solutions to the given problems on
time. Homework solutions should be neat and stapled. Five quizzes, one midterm exam, and
one final exam will be given. The grade distribution of the given assessment tools is shown in
the below table.

Coursework (homework, participation/in-class activities) 20%

--- Page 1 ---
MATH 101 Fundamentals of Mathematical Reasoning
(3 Lecture – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course provides a foundation in logical and mathematical reasoning. It develops first year university
students’ structured logical thinking and mathematical rigor. The course introduces methods of proof,
basic concepts and properties of real numbers, relations and functions. The course also presents an
introduction to combinatorics, set theory and number theory.
Textbook:
• Mathematical Reasoning Writing and Proof by Ted Sundstrom, Open Textbook Initiative, American
Institute of Mathematics, Version 2.1, 2021
Reference Materials:
• Mathematical Proofs: A Transition to Advanced Mathematics by Gary Chartrand,
th
Albert D. Polimeni, Ping Zhang, Publisher: Pearson, 4 Edition, 2017, ISBN: 9780134746753.
Course Topics:
Numbers (1 lecture)
• Introduction to different types of numbers: natural, integers, rational, irrational, real.
Logic I (4 lectures)
• Propositional calculus: truth tables, tautology, contradiction, negation.
Logic II (4 lectures)
• Predicate calculus: implication, equivalence, existential and universal quantifiers.
Methods of Proof (6 lectures)
• Direct method: basic proofs and examples.
• Contrapositive: understanding and applying contrapositive proofs.
• Contradiction: proofs by contradiction and its applications.
Mathematical Induction (3 lectures)
• Induction: principle of mathematical induction, strong induction.
Set Theory Operations on sets (6 lectures)
• Basics of set theory: definition of sets, subset, cardinality.
• Operations on sets: union, intersection, difference, complement.
• Venn diagrams: representing relationships between sets.
Set Theory Properties (3 lectures)
• De Morgan’s Laws: understanding and applying De Morgan's Laws.
• Infinitude of primes: proof and implications.
UCC: March 2024 1

--- Page 2 ---
MATH 101 Fundamentals of Mathematical Reasoning (3 – 0 – 3)
Functions (6 lectures)
• Introduction to relations and functions.
• Injectivity, surjectivity, bijectivity: definitions and examples.
• Composition and inverse of functions.
Number Theory (6 lectures)
• Prime numbers: definition, properties, distribution.
• Divisibility and prime decomposition: fundamental theorem of arithmetic.
• Greatest common divisor (GCD) and least common multiple (LCM).
• Change of base and operations in bases other than 10.
Basic Combinatorics (6 lectures)
• Counting rules: multiplication principle, addition principle.
• Permutations: arrangements, permutations with repetition.
• Combinations: selection, combinations with repetition.
• Binomial theorem: expansion and applications.
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are either 3 x 50 minutes per week or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Lectures will be presented
using the whiteboard, and may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides. Students will
work in groups on a project related to the course topics.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes and assignments) 20%
Project 10%
Presentation 10%
)
Semester Examination 25%
Final Examination 35%

the Final Examination will also be assigned as the grade for the Semester Examination.

Course Catalog Description: This course will introduce students to the theory and techniques of single
variable differential and integral calculus. The emphasis is on problem solving in a science and engineering
context, as opposed to theory. Topics include studying the exponential, logarithmic, trigonometric, and
polynomial functions and their limits, continuity, derivatives, extrema, integration, area under a curve, and
volumes of revolution.
Textbook: Calculus: Early Transcendental Functions by Robert T. Smith, Roland B. Minton and Ziad A. T. Rafhi,
McGraw-Hill, 5th Edition, 2018. ISBN: 9780077166472.
Reference Materials: Calculus, James Stewart, Publisher: CENGAGE Learning, 8th Edition (Metric Version),
2016. ISBN: 9781305266728. Calculus: Concepts & Connections, 1st Edition, by Robert Smith and Roland
Minton, McGraw Hill, 2007. ISBN: 9780073309293.
Syllabus Supplement for Students Page 1 of 5

--- Page 2 ---
Course Learning Outcomes
1 Solve problems involving limits and continuity.
2 Define the derivative of a function and apply the techniques of differentiation.
Combine knowledge of limits, continuity and derivatives in order to classify the important characteristics of
3
a function and draw a graph of the function which illustrates all these characteristics.
4 Apply the techniques of differentiation to solve optimization and related rates problems.
5 Define indefinite integrals and definite integrals and apply them to solving area and volume problems.

Grading Scheme
The passing grade for Calculus 1 is C.
The official Khalifa University of Science and Technology grading system uses letter grades with pluses
and minuses. Passing grades range from A to D; F is failing. Each letter grade is assigned a grade point
on a four-point scale as set out below:
Letter Grade Grade Point Description
A 4.00 Excellent
A- 3.70
Very Good
B+ 3.30
B 3.00
Good
B- 2.70
C+ 2.30
Satisfactory
C 2.00
C- 1.70 Less Than Satisfactory
D 1.00 Poor
F 0.00 Fail
The following is indicative of typical ranges associated with letter grades for the College of Engineering
Undergraduate courses.

Additional Information
Assessments
There are four quizzes scheduled for this course, counting for 20% of the final grade. Homework on
Connect will count for 6%, Tutorial classes count for 9%, Pebble for 5% Midterm for 25% and Final
Exam for 35%.
Calculator Policy
Only basic non-programmable non-graphical calculators are allowed during quizzes and exams.
On campus Classroom Policies
Classes start on time. Students arriving late to class are a distraction to both the instructor and other
students and will therefore be marked absent.
Students are expected to bring for each class what they need for an effective lecture: lecture notes
(posted on Blackboard), pen/pencil, notebook, calculator etc.
Expectations from students
To succeed well in this course, students are required to work at least one hour outside the classroom
for each hour spent in class. That means four hours a week, every week. This is an average
requirement for an average student with a sufficient background. It therefore could be more if some
topics from previous courses are not known well enough.
Selected problems for each section are recommended as homework. Homework will not be collected,
but are essential part of the learning process, and are therefore recommended.
Grading
Writing correct solutions is essential in the life of a scientist, including the correct use of the
mathematical symbols. Not only one needs to be understood by others, but only by paying attention
to what is written a student can acquire a good understanding of the concepts. Therefore, in all
assessments, the correctness of the solution writing will be evaluated and marks will be deducted
for mistakes (notations, clarity, structure of the solution, etc)
For quizzes and midterm, students have TWO DAYS after they get back their copy to check if there are
any grading mistakes, and if so, ask for revision. After two days, the result will be considered final and
no more revision will be allowed.
Conflict Resolution Procedure
For any issue, the following procedure needs to be followed:
1) students need to talk first to their instructor;
2) if (1) did not resolve the issue, students can talk to the course coordinator;
3) if (1) and (2) did not resolve the issue, students can talk to the chair of the math department;
4) if none of the above resolved the issue, students can talk to the Associate dean of undergraduate
studies.
5) No Grade Appeal is approved if the student has not seen the copy of his/her final exam.
There will be no leniency with phones and any other electronics during exams.

--- Page 1 ---
(4 Lecture – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 111 (C grade or higher)
Course Catalog Description:
This is a second semester calculus course for students who have previously been introduced to
the basic ideas of differential and integral calculus. The emphasis in this course is on problem
solving, rather than theory. Topics include integration techniques, parametric equations,
infinite series, an introduction to vectors and vector-valued functions, as well as an introduction
to functions of several variables and double integrals.
Textbook:
Calculus: Early Transcendental Functions by Robert T. Smith, Roland B. Minton and Ziad
A. T. Rafhi, McGraw-Hill, 5th Edition, 2018, ISBN: 9781526869968.
Reference Materials:
 Calculus, James Stewart, Publisher: CENGAGE Learning, 8th Edition (Metric Version),
2016, ISBN: 9781305266728.
 Calculus: Concepts & Connections, 1st Edition, by Robert Smith and Roland Mint
on,
McGraw-Hill, 2007, ISBN: 9780073309293.
Course Topics:
1. Integration techniques (6 lectures)
2. Improper integrals (3 lectures)
3. Parametric equations (4 lectures)
4. Polar coordinates (5 lectures)
5. Sequences of Real Numbers (3 lectures)
6. Introduction to Infinite Series (6 lectures)
7. Infinite series: testing (6 hours)
8. Power series and Taylor series (6 lectures)
9. Vectors and operations with vectors (6 lectures)
10. Planes and surfaces (6 lectures)
11. Vector-valued functions (5 lectures)
12. Introduction to functions of several variables and multiple integrals (5 lectures)
Structure and learning methodologies:
Lectures are 4 x 50 minutes per week. Lectures are presented using the whiteboard, and
may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes and assignments) 30%
Project 10%
Semester Examination 25%
Final Examination 35%

--- Page 1 ---
(4 Lecture – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 111 (C grade or higher)
Course Catalog Description:
This is a second semester calculus course for students who have previously been introduced to
the basic ideas of differential and integral calculus. The emphasis in this course is on problem
solving, rather than theory. Topics include integration techniques, parametric equations,
infinite series, an introduction to vectors and vector-valued functions, as well as an introduction
to functions of several variables and double integrals.
Textbook:
Calculus: Early Transcendental Functions by Robert T. Smith, Roland B. Minton and Ziad
A. T. Rafhi, McGraw-Hill, 5th Edition, 2018, ISBN: 9781526869968.
Reference Materials:
 Calculus, James Stewart, Publisher: CENGAGE Learning, 8th Edition (Metric Version),
2016, ISBN: 9781305266728.
 Calculus: Concepts & Connections, 1st Edition, by Robert Smith and Roland Mint
on,
McGraw-Hill, 2007, ISBN: 9780073309293.
Course Topics:
1. Integration techniques (6 lectures)
2. Improper integrals (3 lectures)
3. Parametric equations (4 lectures)
4. Polar coordinates (5 lectures)
5. Sequences of Real Numbers (3 lectures)
6. Introduction to Infinite Series (6 lectures)
7. Infinite series: testing (6 hours)
8. Power series and Taylor series (6 lectures)
9. Vectors and operations with vectors (6 lectures)
10. Planes and surfaces (6 lectures)
11. Vector-valued functions (5 lectures)
12. Introduction to functions of several variables and multiple integrals (5 lectures)
Structure and learning methodologies:
Lectures are 4 x 50 minutes per week. Lectures are presented using the whiteboard, and
may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes and assignments) 30%
Project 10%
Semester Examination 25%
Final Examination 35%

Instructor Policy:
Quizzes – There will be no make-up quizzes. If the student fails to attend a scheduled quiz without an
approved absence from the Registrar’s Office, then a grade of zero for that quiz will be assigned.
• If the student fails to attend a quiz and the Registrar’s Office confirms that the student's absence from
that quiz is approved, then the grade that will be assigned to that quiz is
– the grade of the Midterm Examination if that quiz took place before the Midterm Examination.
– the grade of the Final Examination if that quiz took place after the Midterm Examination.
Semester Examination – There will be no make-up Semester Examination. If the student fails to attend
the scheduled Midterm Examination without an approved absence from the Registrar’s Office, then a
grade of zero will be assigned for the Semester Examination.
• If the student fails to attend the Semester Examination and the Registrar’s Office confirms that the
absence from the Semester Examination is approved, then the grade for the Final Examination will
also be assigned as the grade for the Semester Examination.

 The scheduling of the classroom quizzes is not flexible. The tests will occur on the dates and time
assigned unless a major change in the academic calendar.
 In case of classes cancelled unexpectedly on a quiz day (bad weather for example), the quiz will
be automatically postponed to the next class.

--- Page 1 ---
MATH 204 Linear Algebra (3–0–3)
MATH 204 Linear Algebra
(3 Lecture – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 112
Course Catalog Description:
This course is an introduction to Linear Algebra, covering matrices, determinants, systems of
linear equations, vector spaces, linear transformations, eigenvalues, and orthogonality. Students
explore solving systems of linear equations, understanding vector spaces, and analyzing linear
transformations. Additionally, they explore the concepts of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, gaining
insight into their significance within linear algebraic structures.
Textbook:
Elementary Linear Algebra by Howard Anton, Publisher: Wiley, 12th Edition, 2019 ISBN:
9781119268048.
Reference Materials:
th
● Linear Algebra: A Modern Introduction by David Poole, Publisher: Brooks Cole, 4
Edition, 2014. ISBN: 9781285463247.
● Differential Equations and Linear Algebra by C. Edwards and D. Penney, Publisher:
Pearson, 4th Edition, 2017. ISBN: 9780134497181.
Course Topics:
1. Matrices, inverses, and Gaussian elimination(6 lectures)
2. Determinants (6 lectures)
3. Vector spaces, linear combinations, and linear dependence (6 lectures)
4. Basis, dimension, subspaces, rank, and nullity (6 lectures)
5. Column space, row space, and null spaces(6 lectures)
6. Linear transformations and matrix representation (5 lectures)
7. Eigenvalues, eigenvectors, and diagonalization (5 lectures)
8. Inner products and orthogonality (3 lectures)
9. Gram-Schmidt process (2 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are either 3 x 50 minutes per week or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Lectures are presented
using the whiteboard and may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes) 40%
Semester Examination 25%
Final Examination 35%
UCC: May 2024

Additional Information:
Assessment
Unreadable and/or poorly written algebraic steps in the solution process will be penalized.
If an answer is given but the working is not shown, then zero credit is awarded.
There are 4 quizzes scheduled in this course. Only the best 3 quiz scores will count towards your final
grade, i.e. the lowest quiz score will be dropped. Each quiz will therefore count for 13.33% of your
final grade for the course.
Calculator Policy
You may only use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic computations in the MATH-
204 course. The calculator must not be able to perform any matrix operations nor have a graphing
capability. A good example of an appropriate calculator is the Casio fx-82ES. Hence, it is not
permitted to use the calculator to perform any operations involving matrices.
You will be permitted to only use a basic calculator in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination and the
Final Examination. Please do not try to use a more powerful calculator in the Quizzes and
Examinations as it will be confiscated.
Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination or Final
Examination. Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put away
for the duration of the quiz/exam.
Classroom Policies
Discipline is one of the main components of your academic and professional success.
Please make every effort to arrive at the class periods in a timely fashion. Students arriving late to
class are a distraction to both the instructor and the other students in the classroom.
Please ensure that you sign your name on the Attendance Sheet which is handed-out at the beginning
of each class period. If you do not sign the Attendance Sheet, then you will be marked “Absent” in
the Banner.
If you are absent for any lecture, it is your responsibility to cover the missed material.

Course Code and Title MATH 211 Differential Equations and Linear Algebra
Semester: Spring 2025

Course Catalog Description: This course introduces ordinary differential equations with linear algebra. The
course covers basic topics of linear algebra, including linear systems, basic properties of matrices, vector
spaces, and eigenvalues and eigenvectors. The course also covers solution techniques for first‐order
differential equations, higher‐order linear equations with constant coefficients, linear and almost linear
systems of differential equations, and Laplace transforms.
Textbook: Differential Equations and Linear Algebra by C. Edwards, D. Penney and David Calvis, Publisher:
th
Pearson, 4 Edition, e‐book, 2017, ISBN: 978‐0134497181.
Reference Materials: Differential Equations and Linear Algebra by Gilbert Strang, Publisher: Wellesley‐
Cambridge, UK edition, 2015, ISBN: 9780980232790.
Syllabus Supplement for Students Page 1 of 5

--- Page 2 ---
Course Learning Outcomes
1 Solve systems of linear equations.
2 Compute eigenvalues, Eigenvectors and Eigenspaces.
3 Apply the concepts of vector spaces to the solution of ordinary differential equations.
4 Solve real world problems using ordinary differential equations and systems of differential equations.
5 Solve initial value problems using Laplace transforms.
Instructor Policy on Late Submission of Assignments
MAKE UP POLICY
• Quizzes – There will be no make‐up quizzes. If the student fails to attend a scheduled quiz without an
approved absence from the Registrar’s Office, then a grade of zero for that quiz will be assigned.
• If the Student Success Department confirms that your absence from a quiz is approved, then the grade you
will receive will either be your Semester Examination grade (Quiz 1 and Quiz 2) or your Final Examination grade
(Quiz 3 and Quiz 4).
• Semester Examination ‐ There will be no make‐up Semester Examination. If you fail to attend the scheduled
Semester Examination without an approved absence from the Student Success Department, then you will
receive a grade of zero for the Semester Examination.
• If the Student Success Department confirms that the absence on the Semester Examination is approved, then
the grade that you achieve for the Final Examination will also be assigned as your grade for the Semester
Examination.
• A maximum of 40% of the course grade can be compensated through the above policy

Grading Scheme
The passing grade for Math 211 is D.
The official Khalifa University of Science and Technology grading system uses letter grades with pluses and
minuses. Passing grades range from A to D; F is failing. Each letter grade is assigned a grade point on a four‐point
scale as set out below:
Letter Grade Grade Point Description
A 4.00 Excellent
A‐ 3.70
Very Good
B+ 3.30
B 3.00
Good
B‐ 2.70
C+ 2.30
Satisfactory
C 2.00
C‐ 1.70 Less Than Satisfactory
D 1.00 Poor
F 0.00 Fail
The following is indicative of typical ranges associated with letter grades for the College of Engineering
Undergraduate courses.
Percentage Grade Range
Letter Grade (rounded)
A 93%‐100%
A‐ 90%‐92%
B+ 87%‐89%
B 83%‐86%
B‐ 80%‐82%
C+ 77%‐79%
C 73%‐76%
C‐ 70%‐72%
D 60%‐69%
F < 60%

Additional Information
Assessments
There are four quizzes scheduled for this course, counting for 20% of the final grade. Online Homework will
count for 10%, a Project for 10%, a Midterm for 25% and a Final Exam for 35%.
Calculator Policy
You may use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic computations in this course. The calculator
must not have a graphing capability. The calculator must not be capable of performing symbolic manipulation.
The calculator must not be capable of computing derivatives and integrals. A good example of an appropriate
calculator is the Casio fx‐82ES.
You will be permitted to use your (basic) calculator in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination and the Final
Examination.
Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination or Final Examination.
Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put away for the duration of the
quiz/exam.
Classroom Policies
Discipline is one of the main components of your academic and professional success. Please make every effort to
arrive at the class periods in a timely fashion. Students arriving late to class are a distraction to both the instructor
and the other students in the classroom. If students require the class periods to end on time then the class periods
must begin on time.
If you are absent from any class period it is your responsibility to make‐up the missed material.
Grading
Writing correct solutions is essential in the life of a scientist, including the correct use of the mathematical
symbols. Not only one needs to be understood by others, but only by paying attention to what is written a
student can acquire a good understanding of the concepts. Therefore, in all assessments, the correctness of the
solution writing will be evaluated and marks will be deducted for mistakes (notations, clarity, structure of the
solution, etc)
For quizzes and midterm, students have TWO DAYS after they get back their copy to check if there are any
grading mistakes, and if so, ask for revision. After two days, the result will be considered final and no more
revision will be allowed.
Conflict Resolution Procedure
For any issue, the following procedure needs to be followed:
1) students need to talk first to their instructor;
2) if (1) did not resolve the issue, students can talk to the course coordinator;
3) if (1) and (2) did not resolve the issue, students can talk to the chair of the math department;
4) if none of the above resolved the issue, students can talk to the Associate dean of undergraduate studies.
5) No Grade Appeal is approved if the student has not seen the copy of his/her final exam.
There will be no leniency with phones and any other electronics during exams.

Teaching Plan
Ch1: First Order Differential Equations
1.1 Differential Equations and Mathematical Models
Week 1 (Jan 13) 1.2 Integrals as General and Particular Solutions

Additional Information:
Assessment
There are 2 quizzes scheduled in this course. Each quiz will therefore count for 10% of your
final grade for the course. There will be no make-up quizzes. If you fail to attend a scheduled quiz
for any reason, then you will receive a grade of zero for that quiz.
Assignment
In the week 3 of the semester, students will be provided an assignment in groups and they will
have to submit the week before the Midterm. Each group will be composed of 3 or 4 students and
will have a different data set. The assignment must be submitted in the week 8 of the semester. If
the report is submitted late, the project grade will be decreased by 10% for each day of delay.
Project
In the week 11 of the semester, students will be assigned a project regarding data analysis using a
statistical software. Each group will be composed of 3 or 4 students and will have a different data
set. A final report on the data analysis must be submitted in the week 15 of the semester. If the
report is submitted late, the project grade will be decreased by 10% for each day of delay.
Calculator Policy
You may use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic calculations. Calculators with
any sort of graphing, symbolic manipulation or integral and derivative calculation capabilities
are strictly prohibited. Any such device found with a student during any form of assessment will
be treated as a violation of the above-stated Honor Code Academic Pledge.
You will be permitted to only use a basic calculator in the Quizzes, Semester Examination and the
Final Examination.
Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Semester Examination or Final
Examination. Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put
away for the duration of the quiz/exam.
Classroom Policies
Discipline is one of the main components of your academic and professional success.
Please arrive at the class periods in a timely fashion. Classes will start as scheduled. Students
arriving late to class are a distraction to both the instructor and the other students in the classroom.
As per the University policies, any student arriving late to class is to be considered absent.
Please ensure that you sign your name on the Attendance Sheet which is handed-out at the
beginning of each class period. If you do not sign the Attendance Sheet, then you will be marked
“Absent” in the Banner.
If you are absent for any lecture, it is your responsibility to cover the missed material.
Homework Policy
You are expected to solve all examples presented in the classroom as well as exercises at the end
of each section of the book. Homework solutions will not be collected-in nor graded. Homework
is your responsibility. Assistance is readily available from the Instructor at his Office Hours.

MATH 244 Probability
(3 Lecture – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 101, MATH 112
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces the mathematical theory of probability at an undergraduate level of rigor. The course
covers basic concepts of axiomatic probability and conditional probability, random variables/vectors and their
distribution, moments, and various models of random variables. Students also study classical probability
inequalities and limit theorems in large sample theory.
Textbook:
nd
Statistical Inference by G. Casella, R.L. Berger, Publisher: Cengage Learning, 2 Edition, (e-book) 2021, ISBN:
9780357753125
Reference Materials:
th
• Introduction to Mathematical Statistics and Applications by R. Larsen, M. Marx, Publisher: Pearson, 6
Edition, 2017, ISBN 9780134114217.
• Probability and Statistics. The Science of Uncertainty by M.J. Evans, J.S. Rosenthal, Publisher: Freeman,
nd
2 Edition, 2009, ISBN: 9781429224628.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to set theory and counting (2 lectures)
2. Probability axioms and simple probability spaces (4 lectures)
3. Conditional probability and independence (3 lectures)
4. Random variables, random vectors and their distributions (6 lectures)
5. Marginal and conditional distributions (4 lectures)
6. Laws of functions of one random variable (2 lectures)
7. Laws of linear combinations of two independent random variables (2 lectures)
8. Laws of the maximum and the minimum for i.i.d. random variables (2 lectures)
9. Expectation, variance and higher order moments; expectation inequalities (4 lectures)
10. Moment generating functions (2 lectures)
11. Common families of distributions (3 lectures)
12. Probability concentration inequalities (2 lectures)
13. Convergence in probability and convergence in distribution (3 lectures)
14. Laws of large numbers, classical central limit theorem and the delta-method (5 lectures)
15. The sample mean and the sample variance: characteristics and distributions (1 lecture)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are either 3 x 50-minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Lectures will be presented using the whiteboard,
and may be complemented with handouts or presentation slides.
UCC: March 2024 1

MATH 251 Operations Research I
(4 Lecture – 4 Credits)
Prerequisites: MATH 204 or MATH 211
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces operations research and deterministic mathematical modeling with a focus
on linear programming. Topics include graphical interpretation of linear optimization problems,
simplex method, duality, sensitivity analysis, and general solution strategies. Emphasis is given to
modeling industrial problems and interpretation of the solutions obtained. Students learn to use
modern modeling languages and software to find optimal solutions for large-scale problems.
Textbook:
Introduction to Operations Research by Hillier, F.S. and Lieberman, G.J, Publisher: McGraw
th
Hill, 11 edition, 2022, ISBN: 9781307810240.
Reference Material:
Operations Research: Applications & Algorithms by Wayne L Winston, Publisher: Cengage
th
Learning, 4 Edition (e-book), 2004, ISBN: 9780357337769.
Course Topics
1. Introduction to operations research and linear programming (5 lectures)
2. Linear optimization modeling: components of optimization models (6 lectures)
3. Linear optimization modeling of real-world problems I (6 lectures)
4. Implement linear optimization models and interpret results (6 lectures)
5. The simplex method (6 lectures)
6. The big M method, two-phase simplex method (4 lectures)
7. The revised simplex method (5 lectures)
8. Duality and sensitivity analysis (6 lectures)
9. Dual simplex method (4 lectures)
10. Transportation and assignment models (6 lectures)
11. Network optimization models (shortest path problem, max flow, min cost) (6 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are either 2 x 75 and 1 x 50 minutes or 2 x 100 minutes per week. Lectures are presented
using the whiteboard, and may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides. The
lectures include problem solving and tutorials with use of software packages. Students work in group
projects to solve and analyze optimization problems.
Software Resources:
LINDO/LINGO, AMPL CPLEX, GUROBI, AIMMS, EXCEL.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes, homework, case studies) 25%
Project 20%
Semester Examination 25%
Final Examination 30%
UCC: March 2024 1

MATH 234 Discrete Mathematics
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 112
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
Topics covered in this course include propositional and predicate calculus, mathematical reasoning
including mathematical induction, an introduction to sets, basic number theory, functions, relations,
graphs, trees, cardinality, counting techniques, linear recurrence relations, and Boolean Algebra.
Textbook:
th
Discrete Mathematics and its Applications, Global Edition by K.H. Rosen, Publisher: McGraw Hill, 7
Edition, 2012. ISBN: 9780071315012.
Reference Materials:
th
Discrete Mathematics with Applications by Susanna S. Epp, Publisher: Brooks Cole, 4 Edition, 2010.
ISBN: 9780495391326.
th

quizzes will occur on the dates and times assigned to them above. Please refer to “Instructor Policy on

Every assessment instrument (Quizzes, Midterm Examination and Final Examination) will include a
“Mathematical Language and Writing” component within the total grade. A number of points,
approximately 10% of the points awarded for the solution of the problems, will be awarded for the
quality and accuracy of the mathematical language and writing that has been used in the solution
process. For example, if the solutions to the problems in a quiz are worth 40 points then an additional
4 points will be allocated for mathematical language and writing, so the quiz will be graded out of a

Calculator Policy
You may use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic computations in the MATH-206
course. The calculator must not have a graphing capability. The calculator must not be capable of
performing symbolic manipulation. The calculator must not be capable of computing derivatives and
integrals. A good example of an appropriate calculator is the Casio fx-82ES.
You will be permitted to use your (basic) calculator in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination and the
Final Examination. Please do not try to use a more powerful calculator in the Quizzes and
Examinations as it will be confiscated.
Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination or Final
Examination. Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put away
for the duration of the quiz/exam.

require the class periods to end on-time then the class periods must begin on-time.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
• Attendance: Mandatory, 3.5% of the final grade accounts for attendance and class participation.
• Active engagement in class evaluation constitutes 0.5% of the overall final grade
• 1% of the final grade is allocated for opportunities such as extra credit and at the instructor's
discretion.
• A student may be absent for no more than 7% of contact time (2 classes) from a course with no
penalty, provided that the student’s absence does not result in him/her missing any items of
assessment (quizzes, exams, turning in homework, etc.).
• For unexcused absences beyond 7% of contact time (after 2 classes), a penalty of 0.5% will be
deducted from your total mark (out of 100%) for each unexcused absence up to 7 classes.

PHYS 121 University Physics I
(3 Lectures, 2 Laboratory, 1 Tutorial – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 111
Course Catalog Description:
This course gives a vector-based and calculus-based introduction to fundamental concepts in
Newtonian mechanics, mechanical conservation laws, oscillations and waves. The course includes
laboratory activities with experiments that demonstrate key concepts discussed in the lectures.
Textbook:
Physics for Scientists and Engineers - Volume 1 by Raymond A. Serway and John W Jewett,
Publisher: Brooks Cole, 9th International Edition, 2014. ISBN-13: 9781285070438 (ISBN-
10:1285070437).
Course Topics:
1. Basic Concepts: Fundamental and derived units, dimensions. (3 lectures)
2. Force and Motion: Velocity, acceleration, and relative motion, Newton’s Laws, friction. (5
lectures)
3. Gravitation: Weight, mass, other types of forces. (2 lectures)
4. Systems of Particles: Centre of mass, linear momentum. (4 lectures)
5. Equilibrium: Requirements, center of gravity, elasticity, elastic moduli. (3 lectures)
6. Work and Energy: Constant and variable force, kinetic and potential energy, conservation of
energy. (4 lectures)
7. Collisions: Elastic and inelastic, impulse, and center of mass. (4 lectures)
8. Rotational Motion: Angular kinematics. (5 lectures)
9. Rotational Dynamics: Torque, Newton’s Laws, angular momentum. (6 lectures)
10. Circular Motion: Newton’s Second Law Applied to uniform circular motion, motion in
accelerated frames. (3 lectures)
11. Oscillations: SHM, resonance, energy considerations. (4 lectures)
12. Mechanical waves. (2 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 3 × 50 minutes, or 2 × 75 minutes, per week. Tutorials will be 1 × 50 minutes per
week. Laboratory will be 1 × 100 minutes per week. Lectures will be presented using both
PowerPoint slides and the whiteboard; copies of PowerPoint slides will be made available to
students.
Laboratory Experiments:
A minimum of 10 laboratory experiments will be conducted from the following list of topics
according to the material covered in the lectures:
1. Units, Measurements and Graphs.
2. Uniformly Accelerated Motion I.
3. Uniformly Accelerated Motion II.
4. Coefficient of Friction.
5. Inelastic Collisions.
6. Elastic Collisions.
7. Force and Torque.
8. Energy Measurements.
9. Centrifugal Force.
10. Hooke’s Law.
11. Simple Harmonic Motion.
May 2025 1

Instructor Policy on Late Submission of Assignments:
• Quizzes: There will be four quizzes on the given date and during the start of the classes i.e. from 11:00 am
to 11:30 am. The grades of all quizzes will be considered for the average, and there will be no makeup
quiz, no extension, or time change for any quiz. If you missed a quiz, it is your responsibility to contact
the student success office and provide them with a legitimate excuse (medical note, for example). You need
to arrange for an email to be sent to the professor (by the student success office). Only then will the missing
quiz be assigned a grade equal to the average of all other quizzes.
• HW: The WebAssign platform will be used for HWs. For each HW, you will be given ample time to finish
it, and there will be no extension or time change for HW. It is important to solve the HW on your own,
without getting help from outside sources. This is an important way to get you ready for the other online
tests.
• Midterm (MT): The MT exam will be on campus and will be scheduled by the registration office. There
will be no make-up MT. The MT test will cover the first seven chapters (Chap 1 to 7). If you missed this
exam and you have a valid excuse, the final exam will carry all the point weight of the combined midterm
and the final exam (e.g., 50%).
• Final Exam: Final Exam will be on-campus and will be scheduled by the registration office.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
There are no make-up quizzes/midterm in this course.
Any absence for an assessment needs to be validated by Student Success Office. If validated:
- for Midterm: The Final Exam result will become the result of the missed Midterm;
- for a quiz: There is no make up for a missed quiz. Student will receive zero credit for any missed quiz.
- for the Final Exam: the official university policy will be applied.
Only Student Success Office can declare an absence justified. Without an email to the instructor from
Student Success Office confirming that the absence is excused, absent students will receive a zero for
the missed assessment.
The scheduling of the classroom quizzes is not flexible. The tests will occur on the dates and time
assigned unless a major change in the academic calendar.

11.4 Approximation by Trigonometric Polynomials Quiz 4
11.7 Fourier Integral
11.8 Fourier Cosine and Sine Transforms
11.9 Fourier Transform. Discrete and Fast Fourier Transforms
Week 16 Final Exams
Additional Information:
Changes will be made to the timetable for the above Teaching Plan if more, or less, time is required for
students to successfully learn the individual topics.
Assessment
There are four quizzes, one midterm exam, and one final exam scheduled for this course.
There will be no make-up tests under any circumstances. In addition, for any online assessment, there
will be up to 20% grade deduction from each assessment in case of failure to follow any of the
Respondus procedure and 0 if there is no video.
Calculator Policy
Only basic calculators with no graphic display will be allowed during quizzes and exams.
Classroom Policies
Lectures start on time. For Online lectures, it is the student’s responsibility to make sure that his/her
computer is working, and the network is available. All technical issues must be addressed to the IT
department. A student that doesn’t log in or did not respond to the call of the instructor will be marked
“absent” in Banner, even if his/her name was shown on BB.
Please do not leave the online class, instructors may take the attendance many times during each period.
Syllabus Supplement for Students Page 4 of 5

--- Page 5 ---
Students are expected to have for each class what they need for an effective lecture: handouts (posted on
Moodle), pen/pencil, notepaper, calculator etc.
Expectations from students
To succeed well in this course, students are required to work at least 1 hour outside the classroom for
each hour spend in class. This is an average requirement for an average student with a sufficient
background. It therefore could be more if some topics from previous courses are not known well enough.
Selected problems for each section are recommended as practice. Practice problems will not be
collected, but are essential part of the learning process, and are therefore mandatory.
Grading
Writing correct solutions is essential in the life of a scientist, including the correct use of the
mathematical symbols. Not only one needs to be understood by others, but only by paying attention to
what is written a student can acquire a good understanding of the concepts. Therefore, in all
assessments, the correctness of the solution writing will be evaluated and marks will be deducted
for mistakes (notations, clarity, structure of the solution, etc)
For quizzes and midterm, students have one week after they get back their copy to check if there are any
grading mistakes, and if so, ask for revision. After one week, the result will be considered final and no
more revision will be allowed.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
There are no make-up quizzes/midterm in this course.
Any absence for an assessment needs to be validated by Student Success Office. If validated:
- for Midterm: The Final Exam result will become the result of the missed Midterm;
- for a quiz: There is no make up for a missed quiz. Student will receive zero credit for any missed quiz.
- for the Final Exam: the official university policy will be applied.
Only Student Success Office can declare an absence justified. Without an email to the instructor from
Student Success Office confirming that the absence is excused, absent students will receive a zero for
the missed assessment.
The scheduling of the classroom quizzes is not flexible. The tests will occur on the dates and time
assigned unless a major change in the academic calendar.

Syllabus Supplement for Students
Course Code and Title CHEG332 Chemical Engineering Thermodynamics II
Semester: Spring 2025

nd

Assessment Methodology
Tentative Dates Weight
Quizzes 3 Quizzes TBA 15%
Laboratory Reports, Quizzes, Presentation Biweekly 20%
Semester Examination (s) Midterm exam Week 8 24%
Final Examination Final exam TBA 32%
Assignments 3 Assignments - 9%
Instructor Policies on Assignments:
Practice problems: they are essential to gain practice in solving thermodynamic problems. Problems
will be assigned biweekly, and students are expected to complete the problems. It is essential that
students do the homework to gain practice in solving the required course problems. I encourage students
to work together in teams on the homework. They can be discussed in the lecture or during help sessions
if there is a need. A good amount of material on tests and the final examination may be based on these
assignments.
Quizzes: These assessments will be designed to give you rapid feedback on how well you understand
the prior week’s topics. Each quiz will last for 75 minutes covering the materials discussed in class and
assigned in the homework. Unless stated otherwise, all quizzes are closed-book but open notes (official
lecture notes). The date of the quiz will be announced beforehand.
Exams: There will be a midterm exam during the semester and a comprehensive final exam. The date
of the exam will be agreed upon at least a week before the assigned time. Exams will be held on a
different location. All Exams–including the final–will be closed-book but open notes (official lecture
notes).
Syllabus Supplement for Students Page 1 of 4

--- Page 2 ---
Lab: Five lab experiments will be conducted in biweekly session. Two weeks after each experiment is
conducted, each group of students must submit a formal lab report. More information on each lab,
description, report, and calculations will be given with the assignment.
Exam re-evaluation: If a student believes that there might be an error in grading a quiz or an exam,
he/she should see the instructor within one (1) week after the assignment/quiz or exam is returned. It
should be noted that the entire exam will be re-graded not just the problem in question, in order to ensure
consistence and fairness. Final course grades may only be contested by following the approved academic
appeal procedures.
A make-up exam will be allowed if approved by the Unievrsity. Repeated exams are normally more
challenging than the ones that are already given.
Attendance
 Students are expected to attend all classes on time. Attendance is required. Students that miss
lectures, tutorials, or other class activities, create significant gaps in their understanding and skill
development. Because of its vital importance, attendance will be recorded and reported to the
Registrar through Banner in each class meeting.
 Lateness to class will not be tolerated. Showing up after 5 minutes of commencing will be
considered as an absence. Leaving class while the instructor is lecturing is considered to be an
impolite behavior. Try not to leave unless it is an emergency.
Highlights of Excused Absences Procedure
 Absences resulting in missing any item of assessment worth up to 5% of the student’s final grade,
should be reviewed by the instructor who makes the final decision regarding missed assessment
grade calculation. In the absence of an official excuse approved by the University, the student
will be assigned a grade of 0% for missing an exam or assignment.

"As a Khalifa University student, I will not lie, cheat, steal, or use any unfair means in academic work
and will behave according to university rules and UAE societal norms and expectations."

LTCM 221 Intercultural Communication 
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)

Prerequisite: ENGL 102 or ENGL 112
Co-requisite: None

Course Catalog Description:
This course identifies and delineates the communication skills needed for effective interaction in a global society; examines the relationship between communication, language and culture; and studies the general concepts of intercultural communication.

Textbook: 
Jandt, F. (2016). An introduction to intercultural communication: Identities in a global community. 8th edition. SAGE Publications, ISBN: 9781506305271

Reference Materials: 
Samovar, P., McDaniel, R. (2017). Communicating between cultures. 9th edition. Cengage ISBN: 9781285444628

Course Topics: 
Introduction to defining culture and communication. (5 lectures)
Perception and intercultural communication. (3 lectures)
Barriers to intercultural communication. (3 lectures)
Intercultural communication and verbal communication. (3 lectures)
Intercultural communication and non-verbal communication. (3 lectures)
Dimensions of culture. (6 lectures)
Comparative cultural patterns. (5 lectures)
Rhetorical patterns and culture. (4 lectures)
History and cultural patterns. (3 lectures) 
Education and diversity. (3 lectures)
Managing intercultural conflict. (3 lectures)
Intercultural communication practices: managing, negotiating and persuading. (4 lectures)

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will either be 3 x 50-minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week.
A combination of white board use, PowerPoint slide presentation, Moodle platform and interactive class discussions to encourage student participation and enhance learning.

Laboratory Experiment Topics: None

Laboratory and/or Computer Resources: None

Assessment: 
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.

Additional Information:
As a general policy and at the discretion of the instructor, students receiving a grade of less than 70% for any assessment will have the opportunity to revise or retake the assessment one time.

Additional Grading Policy Information:

All assignments are due on the date stated by the instructor on this syllabus. Late assignments will be penalized 10% per day (one full letter grade) until submitted.  Assignments submitted after the last day of the course will not be accepted unless announced otherwise.

Students who are found to plagiarize text, images, or other materials at any percentage of the total volume of an assignment will receive one warning and one opportunity to completely revise their work.  A second instance of plagiarism will result in an F on the assignment and a report to the Academic Integrity Committee. Students are advised to consult the Handbook for all policies related to plagiarism and other forms of cheating.

Grade Review Timeframe:
After assessment marks are announced, students have one week to request further clarification of the grade awarded. After the one-week period, no further grade discussions will be considered.

BMED 351 Biomedical Circuits and Systems
(3 Lectures, 3 laboratories – 4 Credits)
Prerequisites: PHYS 122, BMED 212
Course Catalog Description:
The primary objective of this course is to study analogue, digital electronic circuits and their application
to biomedical instrumentation and physiological measurements. The course will focus strongly on
electronic hardware and software design issues required to produce medical instruments, which satisfy
International standards for safety, performance and quality control. Students will be equipped with the
fundamental knowledge required to design Biosignal processing system.
Textbooks:
th
Fundamentals of Electric Circuits by C. K. Alexander & M. N.O. Sadiku, Publisher: McGraw-Hill, 6
International edition, 2016, ISBN: 9781259251320
Reference Materials:
 Medical Instrumentation: Application and Design (4th edition), J. Webster, ed., John Wiley &
Sons, Hoboken, NJ, 2009
 Fredrick M. Cady, Software and Hardware Engineering: Assembly and C programming for the
Freescale HCS12 Microcontroller, Oxford University Press, 2007
Course topics:
1. Electronic circuit elements (resistor, capacitor, inductor, diode, transistor (6 lectures)
2. Ohm’s law, Kirchhoff’s current and voltage laws (8 lectures)
3. Thevenin’s and superposition Theorem for circuit analysis (6 lectures)
4. Transient and steady state analysis of ele ctronic circuit (3 lectures)
5. Operational amplifier and instrumentation amplifier (6 lectures)
6. Design of simple passive and active analogue filters (3 lectures)
7. Signal sampling theory, analogue to digital converter and digital to analogue converter. (5 lectures)
8. Basic Concepts of electronic circuits for safety systems in medical instrumentation Action
Potentials of cells and Bio-potential Electrodes (2 lectures)
9. Biomedical sensor electronics and sensor calibration (2 lectures)
10. Microcomputer system in biomedical devices (4 lectures)
Laboratory experiments:
1) Introduction to Biomedical Circuits Experiment Instruments and circuit components
2) Design and implementation of a simple circuit to demonstrate Kirchoff’s current and voltage laws
3) Design and implementation of a simple circuit to demonstrate Thevenin’s and Superposition
theorem
4) Design and implementation of a practical operational amplifier to amplify and add the two
sinusoidal signals.
5) Demonstration of relationship between voltage and current in diode and transistor
6) Design and implementation of active and passive analogue filters (low pass and high pass)
7) Design of a simple data acquisition system using a microcontroller system by applying the sampling
theorem.
8) Demonstrate the measurement of bio-potentials (heart Electrocardiogram, muscles
Electromyogram, brain Electroencephalogram) through e-health data acquisition system.
9) Demonstrate the measurement of biomedical signals by using biomedical sensors (pressure
transducers for Blood Pressure and Respiratory flow spirometry, condenser microphone for heart
December 2020 1

--- Page 2 ---
BMED 351 Biomedical Circuits and Systems (3-3-4)
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
sound, Infrared light emitting diode for blood flow measurement) through e-health data acquisition
system.
10) Project work on design and implementation of an independent biomedical electronic system
Structure and learning methodologies:
Lectures will be 2x75 minutes and 1x150 minutes laboratory per week. Learning methodology includes
classroom lecture, problem based learning, laboratory experiments and project based collaborative
learning.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes, homework, projects) 25%
Laboratory 25%
Semester Examination(s) 25%
Final Examination 25%
