BUSS 150 Introduction to Economics
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratories – 3 Credits)

Prerequisites: ENGL 112, ENGL 102

Course Catalog Description: 
This course introduces microeconomic concepts and analysis and provides an overview of macroeconomic issues. Topics studied include: the nature and dimensions of competition, the concepts of demand and supply, theories of the firm and individual behavior, market structure, competition and monopoly, costs and incentives, wage determination, and employment, the determination of output, employment, unemployment, interest rates, and inflation. Monetary and fiscal policies are discussed. 

Textbook:
Economics by Michael Parkin, Publisher: Pearson Education, 13th Edition (Global Edition), 2019. Series: MyEconLab. ISBN: 9781292255552

Reference Materials:
URL: www.pearsonmylabandmastering.com
MyEconLab
The Economist

Course Topics: 
An introduction to economics (3 lectures)
Production possibilities and opportunity costs (3 lectures)
The market forces of supply and demand (3 lectures)
Elasticity (3 lectures) 
Economic efficiency and equity (3 lecture) 
Government in markets (3 lectures)
Firms in competitive markets (3 lectures)
Monopoly and monopolistic competition (3 Lectures)
Oligopoly (3 lectures) 
Measuring a nation’s income (3 lectures) 
Measuring the cost of living (3 lectures)
Money, growth and inflation (3 lectures)
13. The exchange rate and balance of payments (3 lectures)
14. Aggregate demand and aggregate supply (3 lectures)

Course Structure and Learning Methodologies: 
Lectures will either be 3 x 50 minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Class will be conducted using PowerPoint-based lectures with problem-solving and in-class exercises. Real world cases and current economic news will be utilized for class discussions. The course uses a mixture of lectures, class discussions and assigned tasks to develop students’ understanding of basic economic concepts and their applications. Bloomberg Market Concept tools and terminals will be engaged in this course (for topics 10 to 15) to enable the student to track, forecast, and understand market and economic changes and concepts. This approach allows students to learn the basics in class and use the Bloomberg Terminal to check their understanding of the course material by completing online assignments.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
No make-up class tests (i.e., Tests 1 to 4 as stated above) will be given. If a student misses a class test, the weight of the missed class test will be transferred to the Final Exam. 
As per University policy, a documented justifiable missed Mid-Semester Exam will receive the same grade as attained on the Final Exam (if the absence on the Mid-Semester Exam is approved by the University). An unapproved absence for the Mid-Semester Exam is not considered a justification and will be graded as a zero.
A make-up Final Exam will only be given if approved by the Registrar’s Office for documented justifiable reasons. An unapproved absence for the Final exam is not considered a justification and will be graded as a zero (i.e. no make-up Final exam will be given without the approval of the Registrar’s Office).

Additional Information:

Tests (30%):
There will be four online-class tests during the semester. All of the tests will be 25 minutes in length and will be given on the tentative dates listed above (subject to change). The tests will cover material you should have mastered since the previous test, but do require you to understand and make use of material taught since the beginning of the course. No make-up tests will be given.

Bloomberg Market Certification (10%):
Bloomberg Market Concepts (BMC) is a self-paced e-learning course that provides an interactive introduction to the financial markets. Students are required to successfully complete only the “Economic Indicators” module under Core Concepts to receive allocated marks for the online assessment component. The BMC scores the subsequent assessment online out of 100, but you will receive either a Pass or Fail grade (i.e. zero or 100%).

Mid-Term Exam (25%):
Mid-Term Examination will be held in Week 7 during class time. No make-up midterm exams will be given. 

Final Exam (35%):
The final exam will cover material from the entire course. The date for the final examination is determined by the Registrar’s Office. No make-up exams will be given, unless approved by the University. 

Calculator Policy:
Non-programmable calculators may be used during the in-class tests and exams. Use of any other electronic device, including programmable calculators, during exams will result in an automatic zero score on the exam. Students are not allowed to share calculators.

BUSS 322 Fundamentals of Innovation & Entrepreneurship
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: Junior Standing
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalogue Description:
This course introduces students to an innovative and entrepreneurial mindset, which will help them develop a creative
problem-solving approach to challenges. Students design an opportunity analysis and a personal business plan, allowing them
to find better solutions to challenges in their career and life.
Textbook:
Technology Ventures: From Idea to Enterprise by Tom Byers, Dorf, and Nelson, Publisher: McGraw Hill Education, 4th Edition,
2015, International Student Edition, ISBN: 9781259875991
Reference Materials:
1. Change by Design: How Design Thinking Transforms Organizations and Inspires Innovation by Tim Brown, Publisher:
HarperCollins, 1st Edition, 2009, ISBN: 9780061766084
2. Creative Confidence: Unleashing the Creative Potential Within Us All by Tom Kelley and David Kelley, Publisher: Crown
Business, 2013, ISBN: 9780385349369
Course Topics:
PHASE I: INTRODUCTION TO INNOVATION
Session 1A: The innovative mindset (1.5 lectures)
Session 1B: Where ideas come from (1.5 lectures)
Session 2A: Creative confidence (1.5 lectures)
Session 2B: The Design Thinking process (1.5 lectures)
Session 3A: Need finding and empathy (1.5 lectures)
Session 3B: Generating insights and reframing (1.5 lectures)
Session 4A: Idea generation (1.5 lectures)
Session 4B: Prototyping and experimentation (1.5 lectures)
Session 5A: Influencing and inspiring others (1.5 lectures)
Session 5B: Leading for creative confidence and going global (1.5 lectures)
Sessions 6A/B: Design Thinking in practice (3 lectures)
PHASE II: INTRODUCTION TO ENTREPRENEURSHIP
Session 7A: The entrepreneurial mindset (vision, mission, and strategy) (1.5 lectures)
Session 7B: How an entrepreneur identifies ideas and opportunities (1.5 lectures)
Session 8A: Lean startup methods I (1.5 lectures)
Session 8B: Lean startup methods II (1.5 lectures)
Session 9A: Go-to-market methods (1.5 lectures)
Session 9B: Opportunity analysis in practice (1.5 lectures)
Session 10A: Sources of capital for entrepreneurs (1.5 lectures)
Session 10B: How entrepreneurs assemble and motivate a Team (1.5 lectures)
Session 11A: Legal matters in startups (1.5 lectures)
Session 11B: Pitching and presenting as an entrepreneur (1.5 lectures)
Sessions 12A/B: Opportunity analysis project (3 lectures)

--- Page 2 ---
BUSS 322 Fundamentals of Innovation & Entrepreneurship (3-0-3)
_________________________________________________________________________________________________________________
PHASE III: INTRODUCTION TO GROWTH & CHANGE
Session 13A: Principles of change and growth (1.5 lectures)
Session 13B: Capturing cross-organizational opportunities (1.5 lectures)
Session 14A: Innovation, success, and failure in large organizations (1.5 lectures)
Session 14B: Reflection on course content (1.5 lectures) Sessions
15A/B: Personal business plan (3 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 2 x 75 minutes per week.
Interactive and participatory learner-centered instructional methods will be utilized to explore content focused themes.
Each session will include a mix of the following components: mini-lectures, discussion, interactive activities, and open Q&A.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (an accumulation of homework tasks and reflections throughout the course) 20%
Project #1: Design Thinking in Practice 20%
Project #2: Opportunity Analysis 20%
Final Project: Personal Business Plan 40%

Syllabus Supplement for Students
Course Code and Title CHEG380 Introduction to Polymer Science and Technology
Semester: Spring 2024
Instructor Name Saeed Alhassan
Contact Email/ Office Ext. No. Saeed.alkhazraji@ku.ac.ae
Office Room No. SAN Campus Arzanah 315
Office Hours Monday 2 PM – 3 PM
Tuesday 11 AM-12 PM
Wednesday 2 PM- 3 PM
Classrooms Section 1: 11:00 AM - 12:15 PM Monday/Wednesday SAN 2137
Section 2: 12:30 PM - 1:45 PM Monday/Wednesday SAN 2137
Course Catalog Description: This course aims to develop a thorough understanding about
polymeric materials including a description of basic organic
chemistry reactions supporting polymerization. Routes to
characterize polymers in terms of molecular weight and molecular
weight distribution are presented. Also, rheological and visco-
elasto plastic properties related to microstructure and
thermomechanical behavior are discussed to establish structure-
property relationships. The prevalence of polymer processing
techniques in various industries is included.
Textbook Fundamental principles of polymeric materials by Stephen L
Rosen, 3rd Edition, Wiley: New York, ISBN: 978-0-470-50542-7,
2012.
Reference Materials Stoyko Fakirov, Fundamentals of Polymer Science for Engineers
(ISBN: 978-3-527-80219-7), 2017
Leslie H. Sperling, Introduction to Physical Polymer Science, 4th
Edition (ISBN: 978-1-119-10374-5), 2012
Assessment Methodology
Tentative Dates Weight
Coursework Quizzes (6, drop 2 lowest) See schedule 15
nd
Project Will be assigned 2 week 15
Exams (2) Week 7 and Week 15 40
Final Exam Finals week 30
General:
• Quizzes will be held in class
• Midterm exam will be held for all students of the two lectures at the same time (more
information will be shared prior to exam time)
• Make-up exams and quizzes will be allowed under extenuating circumstance, such as
hospitalization or bereavement. The excuse must be authenticated by the Student Affairs and
brought to the instructor immediately (within a week) to qualify for permission to make up

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
If the student is absent for a Midterm Exam or the Final Exam, he/she must apply KU rules and regulations to obtain an approval of the excuse.
If a valid excuse is provided for a missed Midterm Exam, the percentage for that exam will be added to the percentage for the Final Exam.
Quiz attendance must be in your own lecture section. You may not attend a quiz in another section.
A mark of zero will be assigned for each missed quiz, whether a valid excuse is presented or not. Please note that no make-up quizzes will be given –quiz grade will be based on the best 4 marks.
For each pre-lab quiz and lab report, failure to submit it by the deadline results in a zero mark. There is no make-up for pre-lab quizzes.
For each lab quiz and lab report, failure to submit it by the deadline will result in a mark of zero. There is no make-up for lab quizzes.
 

Honor Code Academic Pledge:

"As a Khalifa University student, I will not lie, cheat, steal, or use any unfair means in academic work and will behave according to university rules and UAE societal norms and expectations."
 

TEACHING PLAN (Lecture):
Week 1 (15 - 19 January)
 Chapter 1: Structure and Bonding
 
Week 2 (22 - 26 January)
 Chapter 2: Polar Covalent Bonds; Acids and Bases
 
Week 3 (29 January - 2 February)
 Chapter 3: Organic Compounds: Alkanes and Their Stereochemistry
 NOTE: Quiz 1 will take place during this week
 
Week 4 (5 - 9 February)
 Chapter 4 (4.1-4.8): Organic Compounds: Cycloalkanes and Their Stereochemistry 
 
Week 5 (12 - 16 February)
 Chapter 5 (5.1-5.9): Stereochemistry at Tetrahedral Centers
 
Week 6 (19 - 23 February)
 Chapter 6: An Overview of Organic Reactions
NOTE: Quiz 2 will take place during this week
 
Week 7 (26 February - 1 March)
 Chapter 7 (7.1-7.9): Alkenes: Structure and Reactivity
 
Week 8 (4 - 8 March)
 Chapter 8: Alkenes (8.1-8.5): Reactions and Synthesis Reactivity
 
Week 9 (11 - 15 March)
 Chapter 11 (11.1-11.5): Reactions of Alkyl Halides: Nucleophilic Substitutions and Eliminations
NOTE: Midterm will take place during this week
 
Week 10 (18 - 22 March)

 Chapter 15 (15.1-15.5): Benzene and Aromaticity 
Chapter 16 (16.1-16.3): Chemistry of Benzene: Electrophilic Aromatic Substitution
 
Week 11 (1 - 5 April)
 Chapter 17: Alcohols and Phenols (up to 17.1-17.3, 17.6)
Chapter 18: Ethers and Epoxides; Thiols and Sulfides (only 18.1-18.2, 18.8)
NOTE: Quiz 3 will take place during this week
 
Week 12 (8 - 12 April)

 Chapter 19: Aldehydes and Ketones (19.1-19.2, 19.4)

 
Week 13 (15 - 19 April)
 Chapter 20 (20.1, 20.2, 20.7): Carboxylic Acids and Nitriles
 NOTE: Quiz 4 will take place during this week
 
Week 14 (22 - 26 April)
 Chapter 21(21.1-21.2): Carboxylic Acid Derivatives; Nucleophilic Acyl Substitution Reactions
 
Week 15 (29 April - 3 May)
 Chapter 24 (24.1-24.3): Amines and Heterocycles

 Experiment 7: Resolution of racemic mixtures – Part 2

 No Lab – Eid Week

 Experiment 10: Synthesis of Aspirin

 Lab Final Exam

Course Learning Outcomes

Logistics – Examinations
1. Quizzes has been announced in advance. Missing any examination (e.g., quiz, midterm,
etc.) will result in a grade of 0.
2. In case a student has a justification for the absence (e.g., medical report), this should be
directed to the registration office (AND NOT THE INSTRUCTOR) per the university
policy guidelines.
3. The registration will then contact the instructor to inform him/her whether the justification
was accepted or not. Only in that case, the portion of the grade for the examination (e.g.,
5% for a quiz) will be moved to the grade of the final exam.
4. There will be no make-ups exams.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
• If the student is absent for a Midterm Exam or the Final Exam, he/she must apply KU rules and
regulations to obtain an approval of the excuse.
• If a valid excuse is provided for a missed Midterm Exam, either the percentage for that exam will
be added to the percentage for the Final Exam or a make-up exam will be scheduled.
• Quiz attendance must be in your own lecture section. You may not attend a quiz in another section.
• Unless a valid excuse is provided, a mark of zero will be assigned for missed quizzes.
For each lab report, failure to submit it by deadline results in zero mark. There is no make-up for pre-
lab quizzes. Cheating in a lab report results in zero.

KU Grading System – Policy ACA 3350
The official Khalifa University of Science and Technology grading system uses letter grades with pluses
and minuses. Passing grades range from A to D; F is a Fail grade. Each letter grade is assigned a grade
point on a four-point scale as set out below:

Additional letter grades are used by the Registrar to denote special cases. These letter grades do not have
corresponding grade points and are not used in calculating a student’s grade point average.

COSC 202 Data Science and Artificial Intelligence 
(2 Lecture 3 Laboratory– 3 Credits) 
 
Prerequisite: COSC 114 
 
Course Catalog Description: 
The course is designed to give engineers a comprehensive understanding of the key concepts, techniques, tools, and applications of data science and Artificial Intelligence (AI). The course focuses on applications in various engineering domains. Course topics include data analysis and visualization, machine learning, deep learning, applications, natural language processing, computer vision, autonomous driving, and ethics considerations. Students will learn how these concepts can be applied to real-world problems and gain hands-on experience with popular AI and data science tools and libraries. 

Textbook: 

Artificial Intelligence with Python, by Alberto Artasanchez and Prateek Joshi. Publisher: Packt Publishing Ltd, 2nd Edition, 2020. ISBN-10: 183921953X. ISBN-13: 978-1839219535.

Reference Materials: 
Introduction to Machine Learning with Python: A Guide for Data Scientists by Andreas C. Müller and Sarah Guido. Publisher: O'Reilly Media, 1st edition, 2016, ISBN-10: 1449369413, ISBN-13: 978-1449369415.
First Principles with Python by Joel Grus, Data Science from Scratch. Publisher: O’Reilly Media; 2nd Edition, 2019. ISBN-10: 1492041130. ISBN-13: 978-1492041139.

Course Topics: 
Introduction to Computer Science, Data Science and AI: History of Data Science and AI, Data Science and AI Applications and Trends (2 lectures)
Data types and structures: Numerical data, categorical data, text data, data structures such as arrays, tables, and graphs (2 lectures) 
Introduction to fundamentals of data science: Problem-solving with data, data collection, data pre-processing, exploratory data analysis, data acquisition, data cleaning, examples of features, and data visualization (6 lectures) 
Introduction to machine learning: supervised learning with applications, unsupervised learning with applications (4 lectures) 
Introduction to deep learning: introduction to neural networks, application of Conventional Neural Networks (CNN), applications of Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN) (4 lectures)
Introduction to Natural Language Processing: Application 1: Text processing, Application 2: Sentiment analysis (4 lectures)
Applications of AI and Data Science: Computer vision, Speech recognition, Autonomous driving (6 lectures)
Ethics and Privacy in AI and Data Science: Bias in Data and Algorithms, Privacy and Security (2 lectures)

Course Structure and Methodology: 
Lectures are 2 x 50 minutes; Laboratory sessions are 1 x 150 min per week. The course is delivered through classroom lectures, problem-solving sessions, and laboratory work. Individual assignments and quizzes are held throughout the semester to reinforce knowledge of the material.

COSC 330 Introduction to Artificial Intelligence
(2 Lecture, 3 Laboratory – 3 Credits)

Prerequisite: COSC 202, MATH 204 or Math 211, COSC 230 

Course Catalog Description:
This course allows the students to explore the foundational principles and contemporary advancements in Artificial Intelligence (AI). It presents the historical evolution of AI alongside practical problem-solving methodologies like search algorithms and knowledge representation. The course covers intelligent agents, decision trees, classifiers, in addition to some advanced topics such as game theory, to gain a holistic understanding of AI's diverse applications and methodologies. 

Textbook:
Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach by Peter Norvig and Stuart Russell. 4th Edition, 2021,ISBN-10 – 1292401133 ; ISBN-13 - 978-1292401133

Reference Materials:
Artificial Intelligence: A System Approach by M. Tim Jones. Publisher: Jones and Barlett Publishers, 1st Edition, 2008, ISBN: 9780763773373.

Artificial Intelligence, Structures and Strategies for Complex Problem Solving by George F. Luger. Publisher: Addison – Wesley, 6th Edition, 2008 , ISBN-13: 978-0321545893

Course Topics:
AI: General introduction and overview (2 lectures)
Intelligent agents and distinguished environments. ( 2 lectures)
Uninformed Search Techniques (3 lectures)
Informed search techniques (4 lectures)
Knowledge/rule-based representation and reasoning (2 lectures)
Decision tree (4 lectures)
Classification: linear classifier (3 lectures)
Naïve Bayes Classifier (3 lectures)
Artificial Neural Networks: Multi-Layer Perceptron, Backpropagation algorithm. (4 lectures) 
Game theory (3 lectures)

Course Structure:
Lectures are 2 × 50 minutes per week. The laboratory session is 1 × 150 minutes per week. Every newly taught topic is followed by a tutorial and a problem-solving session to encourage student participation. Coursework comprises quizzes and mini-project homework to provide students with a self-learning environment. Laboratory sessions follow the lectures to provide practical examples of theoretical topics.

Lab Work:
Review Python
Review Python: Tree and Graph processing 
Search-1
Search-2
Linear classifier
Bayesian Classification
Artificial Neural Network
Decision tree 
Game Theory
Computer Resources:
Workstations (both Windows and Linux/Unix OS) equipped with powerful GPU cards.

TEACHING PLAN (Lecture):
Teaching Plan
Week 1 Introduction: Automata, computability and complexity; mathematical background;
mathematical proofs (Sec. 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4)
Week 2 Finite automata: examples; definitions; designing finite automata (Sec. 1.1)
Week 3 Nondeterministic finite automata; regular expressions (Sec 1.2, 1.3)
Week 4 Pumping lemma; context free grammars (Sec 1.4, 2.1)
Week 5 Pushdown automata; Turing machines (Sec 2.2, 3.1)
Week 6 Variants of Turing machines and algorithms (Sec. 3.2, 3.3)
Week 7 Decidable languages (Sec 4.1)
Week 8 Decidable languages (Sec 4.1)
Week 9 Reducibility: Undecidable Problems from Language Theory, Reductions via
computation histories (Sec. 5.1)
Week 10 Post correspondence problem (Sec. 5.2)
Week 11 Mapping reducibility; computable functions (Sec. 5.3)
Week 12 The recursion theorem (Sec. 6.1)
Week 13 Measuring complexity; the class P (Sec. 7.1, 7.2)
Week 14 The class NP; NP-completeness (Sec 7.3, 7.4)
Week 15 Additional NP-complete problems (Sec. 7.4, 7.5)
This is a tentative schedule and may be adjusted as required.
NOTE: The Teaching Plan is subject to change, please check regularly for updates in RED font.

BMED 352 Fundamentals of Biomedical Signal Processing
(3 Lectures, 3 Laboratories – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: BMED 351
Course Catalog Description:
The primary objective of this course is to study analogue and digital signal processing techniques
and microcomputer system, and their application to biomedical instrumentation and physiological
measurements. This course is designed for students who are expected to have prior knowledge in
circuits and physiological system modelling. The main focus is on the technical aspects of
biosignal processing and its hardware implementation in medical instruments.
Textbooks:
Discrete-Time Signal Processing by Alan V. Oppenheim, Ronald W. Schafer, Publisher: Prentice
Hall, ISBN: 9781292025728, Pearson New International Edition, 2013.
Reference Materials:
• Oppenheim, Alan, and Alan Willsky. Signals and Systems. 2nd ed. Upper Saddle River, NJ:
Prentice Hall, 1996. ISBN: 9780138147570.
• S. K. Mitra, Digital Signal Processing: A Computer-Based Approach, McGraw-Hill, 4th
edition, 2011. ISBN: 978-007-128946.
• Medical Instrumentation: Application and Design (4th edition), J. Webster, ed., John Wiley &
Sons, Hoboken, NJ, 2009
• Bruce, Eugene N. Biomedical signal processing and signal modelling. Published New York:
Wiley,
• Fredrick M. Cady, Software and Hardware Engineering: Assembly and C programming for
the Freescale HCS12 Microcontroller, Oxford University Press, 2007
Course topics:
1. Introduction to continuous signals (4 lectures)
2. Fourier transform of continuous signal (3 lectures)
3. Practical issues of sampling and discretization of continuous signal (2 lectures)
4. Introduction to discrete signals (2 lectures)
5. Discrete Fourier Transform and frequency responses (4 lectures)
6. Z-transform and transfer function representation of discrete time system (6 lectures)
7. Stability and transient response of transfer function (2 lectures)
8. Closed loop control system (3 lecture)
9. Discrete system convolution (finite and infinite response systems) (2 lectures)
10. Design of Digital filters (4 lectures)
11. Noise removal and signal enhancement (5 lectures)
12. Examples of AI based models in biomedical devices (2 lectures)
13. Functional organization of a microcontroller (2 lectures)
14. Functional organization of a medical device (2 lectures)
15. Practical Issues in implementing digital signal processing systems in medical devices (2
lectures)
Laboratory experiments:
1. Introduction to MATLAB signal processing toolbox and Simulink
2. Design of a simple discrete system in MATLAB Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) to analyze
frequency spectrum of bio signals
November 2021 1

BMED 352 Fundamentals of Biomedical Signal Processing (3-3-4)

3. Design of a digital filter for bio signals using MATLAB
4. Practical realization of a discrete system for processing bio signals using MATLAB
5. Design of a MATLAB/Simulink model for cardiovascular and cardiorespiratory closed loop
control system.
6. Design a simple microcontroller based system for implementing a digital filter for bio signal
measurement and processing
7. Projects works on designing a discrete signal processing system for a physiological function
and realizing the same in a microcontroller platform.
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 3x50 or 2x75 minutes and laboratory will be 1x150 minutes per week. Learning
methodology includes classroom lecture, problem based learning, laboratory experiments and
project based collaborative learning.

COSC 114 Introduction to Computing - Python
(3 Lecture, 3 Laboratory – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces computer systems, including computer hardware components, operating systems,
compiling, debugging, libraries, and linking. It focuses on programming-based problem-solving that
covers the program development lifecycle. It includes imperative programming such as data types,
conditional expressions and statements, repetitive structures, arithmetic and logic operators, functions,
arrays, strings, data structures (database, tuple), and file Input/Output (I/O).
Textbook:
Python Programming: Using Problem-Solving Approach by R. Thareja, Publisher: Oxford University
Press, 2017, ISBN: 978-0-19-948017-3
Reference Materials:
How to Think Like a Computer Scientist: Learning with Python 3. Documentation Release 3rd Edition,
Peter Wentworth, Jeffrey Elkner, Allen B. Downey and Chris Meyers, April 2018 (available online:
https://media.readthedocs.org/pdf/howtothink/latest/howtothink.pdf)
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to computer units and computer language: Machine language, Assembly Language,
and High-level language. (3 lectures)
2. Introduction to Python programming environments. (1 lecture)
3. Syntax and semantics concepts, memory concepts, basic data type declarations and their ranges,
output formatting for data types, assignments, arithmetic and logic operators, operators’
precedence, and associativity. (3 lectures)
4. Problem-solving approaches and algorithms using pseudocode and flowchart. (3 lectures)
5. Control structures I (selections): if, else, elif, selection structures. (3 lectures)
6. Control structures II (repetitions): for, and while loop repetition structures, counter controlled vs.
Sentinel-controlled loop structures, nested loops, break and continue program control statements.
(4 lectures)
7. Functions: passing information to and from functions, scope and visibility of identifiers, iterative
vs. recursive functions. Built-in functions and user-defined functions. (5 lectures)
8. Lists: declaring, passing to functions, linear search, multi-dimensional, sorting. Manipulating of
Lists 1D and 2D. (5 lectures).
9. Strings: Fundamentals of characters and strings, string manipulation functions in Python (ex: find,
len, etc.), string user-defined functions (ex: upper, count, etc.). (4 lectures)
10. Data Structures: dictionary, tuple, passing and returning to/from function. (4 lectures)
11. File I/O: streams insertion and extraction, stream manipulators, open, read, write, and update. (4
lectures)
12. Introduction to Scientific computing using NumPy (3 lectures)
Course Structure and Methodology:
Lectures are 3 x 50 minutes per week; Laboratory session is 1 x 150 min per week
May 2024

ENGR 114 Introduction to Computing - Python
(3 Lectures, 1 Tutorial, 2 Laboratories – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
Introduction to computer systems: computer hardware components, operating system, compiling,
debugging, libraries, linking. Programming based problem solving that includes program development
lifecycle. Imperative programming: data types, conditional expressions and statements, repetitive
structures, arithmetic and logic operators, functions, arrays, strings, data structures (database, tuple),
file I/O.
Textbook:
Python Programming: Using Problem Solving approach by R. Thareja, Publisher: Oxford University
Press, 2017, ISBN: 978-0-19-948017-3
Reference Materials:
How to Think Like a Computer Scientist: Learning with Python 3 Documentation Release 3rd Edition,
Peter Wentworth, Jeffrey Elkner, Allen B. Downey and Chris Meyers, April 2018 (available online:
https://media.readthedocs.org/pdf/howtothink/latest/howtothink.pdf)
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to computer units and computer language: Machine language, Assembly Language
and High level language. (3 lectures)
2. Introduction to Python programming environment (IDLE, Wing IDE 101). (1 lecture + 1 lab)
3. Syntax and semantics concepts, memory concepts, basic data type declarations and their ranges,
output formatting for data types, assignments, arithmetic and logic operators, operators’
precedence, associativity. (3 lectures + 1 Lab)
4. Problem solving approaches, algorithms using pseudocode and flowchart. (3 lectures + 1 Lab)
5. Control structures I (selections): if, else, elif, selection structures. (3 lectures + 1.5 Labs)
6. Control structures II (repetitions): for, and while loop repetition structures, counter controlled vs.
sentinel controlled loop structures, nested loops, break and continue program control statements.
(4 lectures + 1.5 Labs)
7. Functions: passing information to and from functions, scope and visibility of identifiers, iterative
vs. recursive functions. Built in functions and user defined functions. (5 lectures + 2 Labs)
8. Lists: declaring, passing to functions, linear search, multi-dimensional, sorting. Manipulating of
Lists 1D and 2D. (4 lectures + 1 Lab).
9. Strings: Fundamentals of characters and strings, string manipulation functions in Python (ex: find,
len, etc.), string user defined functions (ex: upper, count, etc.). (4 lectures + 1 Lab)
10. Data Structures: dictionary, tuple, passing and returning to/from function. (4 lectures + 1 Lab)
11. File I/O: streams insertion and extraction, stream manipulators, open, read, write and update. (3
lectures + 1 Labs)
12. Scientific computing using NumPy arrays, Pandas DataFrames and Python plotting (matplotlib,
seaborn) (3 lectures + 2 labs)
13. Basic introduction to Artificial Intelligence (2 lectures + 1 lab)
Course Structure:
Lectures will be 3 x 50-minutes per week; Laboratory sessions will be 1 x 100 min per week; Tutorial
sessions will be 1 x 50 min per week.
Computer Resources:
Computer lab equipped with IDE environments such as IDLE and Wing IDE 101,
Laboratory/Assignments Topics:

Syllabus Supplement for Students Page 1 of 2

--- Page 2 ---
TEACHING PLAN (Lectures):
Week 1 Equilibrium of a Particle and Force System Resultants
Week 2 Equilibrium of a Particle and Force System Resultants
Week 3 Equilibrium of a Rigid Body
Week 4 Equilibrium of a Rigid Body
Week 5 Structural Analysis
Week 6 Internal Forces
Week 7 Center of Gravity, Centroid, and Moment of Inertia
Week 8 Kinematics of a Particle
Week 9 Kinetics of a Particle: Force, Acceleration, Work, and Energy
Week 10 Kinetics of a Particle: Impulse and Momentum; Stress and Strain
Week 11 Stress and Strain; Mechanical Properties of Materials
Week 12 Group Project
Week 13 Group Project
Week 14 Group Project
Week 15 Project Presentations, Final Exam Review
Course Learning Outcomes
• Draw free-body diagrams and solve for forces and moments in both static and dynamic systems
• Understand and describe the various mechanical properties of materials
• Describe and analyze the motion of particles and rigid bodies
• Identify, formulate and solve problems involving kinematic/kinetic variables using equations of
motion, principles of impulse and momentum, and principles of work and energy
• Obtain stress-strain distributions in organic and synthetic materials under various loading modes
• Work in teams and communicate effectively
Book
“Statics and Mechanics of Materials in SI Units” 5th Edition, Russell C. Hibbeler
Supplementary course notes may be provided in addition to the text book where necessary.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:

to make up any exam missed.

TEACHING PLAN (Lecture):
Teaching Plan Introduction:
Week 1 Basic Concepts, Units used & Conversions, and Fluid Physical properties; (Chapter 1
textbook)
Week 2 Fluid Statics: Fundamentals of Fluid Statics: (Chapter 2 textbook)
Week 3 The Balance Equation: Mass Balance; (Chapter 3 textbook)
Week 4 The Balance Equation: Energy Balance; (Chapter 4 textbook)
Week 5 Bernoulli’s Equation; Chapter 5 (textbook)
Week 6 Examples on Bernoulli’s Equation; (Chapter 5 textbook)
Week 7 Fluid Friction in Steady flow(Chapter 6 textbook)
Week 8 Midterm Exam
Week 9 Examples on Fluid Friction (Designing liquid piping systems) and General Revision
Week 10 The Momentum Balance; (Chapter 7 textbook)
Week 11 High-Velocity Gas flow; (Chapter 8, textbook)
Week 12 Spring Break
Week 13 Models, Dimensional Analysis, and Dimensional Numbers (Chapter 9 textbook)
Week 14 Pumps, compressors, and Turbines (Chapter 10 textbook)
Week 15 Pumps, compressors, and Turbines (Chapter 10 textbook)
Week 16 Introduction to flow through porous Media (Chapter 11 textbook)

GENS 100 Academic Development and Success
(1 Lecture, 0 Laboratory – 1 Credit)
Prerequisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This interactive student success course helps students apply positive behavioral principles through their
university experience and beyond. The course delivers a blended learning experience designed to enhance
students’ self-awareness, orient them to KU resources and learning communities, make major declaration
decisions, and adopt academic integrity. Students develop the growth mindset necessary to succeed in a
global and ever-changing marketplace.
Textbook: None
Reference Materials: 7 Habits of Highly Effective College Students by Sean Covey, Publisher:
Franklin Covey, Essentials Edition, 2014. ISBN-10: 1936111616/ ISBN- 978-1936111619.
Course Topics:
1) Introduction to course (1 lecture)
2) Practical application of skills- Accessing university resources (1 lecture)
a) MS Outlook and Blackboard LMS platforms
b) KU Portal and Navigate
3) Self-awareness and growth mindset (2 lectures)
a) Online personality assessment and learning styles
b) Transition from high school to university
4) Developing skills in collaboration/teamwork- Experiential learning and Reflective Practice (2
lectures)
a) Extracurricular: clubs and societies (‘Find your tribe’)
b) Leadership skills development- Community service, peer tutoring/mentoring (CTL/Hive)
c) D.E.E.P Reflection Model
d) E-Portfolio: document experience, Reflections and achievements
5) SMART long and short-term academic goals (2 lectures)
a) Mission statement
b) Attributes of work ethic- Managing academic priorities
6) Academic Success Plan (1 lecture)
7) Study skills (3 lectures)
a) Time and attention management
b) Priority Management
c) Revision and retrieval strategies
8) Major Declaration/Exploration (1 lecture)
9) Attributes in being ethically responsible (2 lectures)
a) Ethical Use of AI Tools- Chat GPT, resource or cheating tool
b) Referencing & citation (plagiarism)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
Quizzes – There are no make-up quizzes. If you fail to attend a scheduled quiz without an approved
absence from the Student Services, then you will receive a grade of zero for that quiz. If the Student Services
confirm that your absence from a quiz is approved, then the grade you receive will either be your Semester
Examination grade (Quiz 1, Quiz 2, and Quiz 3) or your Final Examination grade (Quiz 4 and Quiz 5).
Semester Examination – There are no make-up Semester Examination. If you fail to attend the scheduled
Semester Examination without an approved absence from the Student Services, then you will receive a
grade of zero for the Semester Examination. If the Student Services confirm that the absence on the Semester
Examination is approved, then the grade that you achieve for the Final Examination will also be assigned as
your grade for the Semester Examination.

GENS 100 Academic Development and Success
(1 Lecture, 0 Laboratory – 1 Credit) 

Prerequisite: None

Course Catalog Description: 
This interactive student success course helps students apply positive behavioral principles through their university experience and beyond. The course delivers a blended learning experience designed to enhance students’ self-awareness, orient them to KU resources and learning communities, make major declaration decisions, and adopt academic integrity. Students develop the growth mindset necessary to succeed in a global and ever-changing marketplace.

Textbook: None

Reference Materials: 7 Habits of Highly Effective College Students by Sean Covey, Publisher: Franklin Covey, Essentials Edition, 2014. ISBN-10: 1936111616/ ISBN- 978-1936111619.

Course Topics: 
Introduction to course (1 lecture)
Self-awareness and growth mindset (2 lectures) 
Online personality assessment and learning styles
Transition from high school to university 
Major Declaration Program (2 lectures)
Introduction to process
Identifying career choices
Practical application of skills- Accessing university resources (2 lectures) 
MS Outlook and Blackboard LMS platforms 
KU Portal and Navigate
Developing skills in collaboration/teamwork- Experiential learning communities (2 lectures) 
Extracurricular: clubs and societies (‘Find your tribe’)
Leadership skills development- Community service, peer tutoring/mentoring (CTL/Hive)
E-Portfolio: document experience and achievements 
SMART long and short-term academic goals (2 lectures) 
Mission statement
Attributes of work ethic- Managing academic priorities
Attributes in being ethically responsible (2 lectures) 
AI Tech- Chat GPT- resource or cheating tool
Referencing & citation (plagiarism)
Study skills (2 lectures) 
Time and attention management
Revision and retrieval strategies

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are 1 x 50 minutes each week, delivered in either a classroom or experiential setting. Students will be required to have access to Blackboard to view or download course-specific material or upload documented evidence of participation in planned experiential learning events and activities. Meetings with instructors for tutorial sessions will also require documentation when necessary.

Laboratory and/or Computer Resources: Access to KU Portal, Blackboard LMS, Navigate, CTL. 

Assessment: All Course Learning Outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
Quizzes – There are no make-up quizzes. If you fail to attend a scheduled quiz without an approved absence from the Student Services, then you will receive a grade of zero for that quiz. If the Student Services confirm that your absence from a quiz is approved, then the grade you receive will either be your Semester Examination grade (Quiz 1, Quiz 2, and Quiz 3) or your Final Examination grade (Quiz 4 and Quiz 5).
Semester Examination – There are no make-up Semester Examination. If you fail to attend the scheduled Semester Examination without an approved absence from the Student Services, then you will receive a grade of zero for the Semester Examination. If the Student Services confirm that the absence on the Semester Examination is approved, then the grade that you achieve for the Final Examination will also be assigned as your grade for the Semester Examination.

Additional Information:
Assessment
There are 5 quizzes scheduled in this course. Four best quiz scores will count towards your final grade. The lowest quiz score will be dropped. No makeup will be granted for quizzes under any circumstance. Any unexcused absence to a quiz will result in a grade of 0% for that quiz.

Case study
Students will work in groups of 3 or 4 on a case study related to modelling and application of the scholastic processes theory learnt in the course. A report shall be submitted in week 15. For every late day, 5% of the grade will be deducted.

Mobile phone Policy

Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Semester Examination or Final Examination. Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put away for the duration of the quiz/exam.

Calculator Policy
You may use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic calculations.
Calculators with any sort of graphing, symbolic manipulation or integral and derivative calculation capabilities are strictly prohibited. Any such device found with a student during any form of assessment will be treated as a violation of the above-stated Honor Code Academic Pledge.

Classroom Policies

Discipline is one of the main components of your academic and professional success.

Please make every effort to arrive at the class periods in a timely fashion. Students arriving late to class are a distraction to both the instructor and the other students in the classroom. As per the Univ

GENS 300 Career Preparation
(1 Studio, 1 Credit)
Prerequisite: GENS 100, Sophomore Standing
Course Catalog Description:
This course equips learners with the mindset and skills for both internships and future careers. It includes
employability workshops, group discussions, and one-on-one guidance from a local employability and
labor market specialist. This activity-based course is focused on field exploration, CV and digital profile
development, and professional communication. Course delivery is a blend of in-person and asynchronous
online learning experiences.
Textbook: None
Reference Materials: What Color is Your Parachute for College by Katharine Brooks, Publisher: Ten
Speed Press, 2021, ISBN: 9781984857569.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to the course (1 lecture)
2. Preparing skills for employability: Knowledge, experience, and personal attributes (1 lecture)
3. Emerging professional profile: Researching and writing a bespoke CV (1 lecture)
4. Interview skills development (2 lectures)
a. Communication- active listening
b. Resilience (responding to feedback)
5. Job (or internship) search techniques (1 lecture)
a. Setting SMART career goals
6. Experiential learning communities (2 lectures)
a. On-campus employment opportunities
b. Updating industry-based e-Portfolio
7. Creating an online professional profile (2 lectures)
a. Networking through LinkedIn and Glassdoor
b. Trends in the UAE job market
8. Teamwork: skills and strategies (2 lectures)
a. Effective communication and empathy
b. Team building and collaboration
c. Critical thinking and decision-making
9. Work ethic and business etiquette (2 lectures)
a. Professionalism and self-discipline
b. Reflection on changed paradigm, and enhance the proactive skill set
10. Intercultural attributes: Adaptable in changing environments (1 lecture)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Studio sessions are 1 x 100 minutes per week. Sessions involve interactive activities in a blended learning
environment that includes online lessons and classroom lectures facilitated by the course instructor.
Assessments will be formative via, online knowledge checks, digital portfolios, peer assessment,, and an
online summative assessment.
UCC: March 2023 1

GENS 300 Career Preparation
(1 Studio, 1 Credit)
Prerequisite: GENS 100, Sophomore Standing
Course Catalog Description:
This course equips learners with the mindset and skills for both internships and future careers. It includes
employability workshops, group discussions, and one-on-one guidance from a local employability and
labor market specialist. This activity-based course is focused on field exploration, CV and digital profile
development, and professional communication. Course delivery is a blend of in-person and asynchronous
online learning experiences.
Textbook: None
Reference Materials: What Color is Your Parachute for College by Katharine Brooks, Publisher: Ten
Speed Press, 2021, ISBN: 9781984857569.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to the course (1 lecture)
2. Preparing skills for employability: Knowledge, experience, and personal attributes (1 lecture)
3. Emerging professional profile: Researching and writing a bespoke CV (1 lecture)
4. Interview skills development (2 lectures)
a. Communication- active listening
b. Resilience (responding to feedback)
5. Job (or internship) search techniques (1 lecture)
a. Setting SMART career goals
6. Experiential learning communities (2 lectures)
a. On-campus employment opportunities
b. Updating industry-based e-Portfolio
7. Creating an online professional profile (2 lectures)
a. Networking through LinkedIn and Glassdoor
b. Trends in the UAE job market
8. Teamwork: skills and strategies (2 lectures)
a. Effective communication and empathy
b. Team building and collaboration
c. Critical thinking and decision-making
9. Work ethic and business etiquette (2 lectures)
a. Professionalism and self-discipline
b. Reflection on changed paradigm, and enhance the proactive skill set
10. Intercultural attributes: Adaptable in changing environments (1 lecture)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Studio sessions are 1 x 100 minutes per week. Sessions involve interactive activities in a blended learning
environment that includes online lessons and classroom lectures facilitated by the course instructor.
Assessments will be formative via, online knowledge checks, digital portfolios, peer assessment,, and an
online summative assessment.

HUMA 229 - Critical Thinking
(3 Lecture, – 3 Credits) 
 
Prerequisite: ENGL 102
Co-requisite: None
 
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces students to the principles and practices of critical thinking. Through analysis, evaluation, and reasoning, students will help develop the cognitive skills and dispositions necessary to engage in clear, logical, and independent thought. Emphasis will be placed on applying these skills in academic, professional, and everyday contexts.
 
Textbooks: 
R. Paul and L. Elder, Critical Thinking: Tools for Taking Charge of Your Professional and Personal Life. Upper Saddle River, NJ: Financial Times Prentice Hall, 2002. ISBN: 0-13-064760-8.

Reference Materials: 
IEEE Editorial Style Manual (available online: https://www.ieee.org/content/dam/ieee-org/ieee/web/org/conferences/style_references_manual.pdf)
University of Pittsburgh Library System: Citation Styles (available online: https://pitt.libguides.com/citationhelp)
R. Gula, Nonsense: Red Herrings, Straw Men and Sacred Cows: How We Abuse Logic in Our Everyday Language. Virginia: Axios Press, 2018.
G. Graff and C. Birkenstein, They Say / I Say: The Moves That Matter in Academic Writing, 5th ed. New York, NY: W. W. Norton & Company, 2021.
J. C. Bean, Engaging Ideas: The Professor's Guide to Integrating Writing, Critical Thinking, and Active Learning in the Classroom. San Francisco, CA: Jossey-Bass, 2001.
S. Brookfield, Teaching for Critical Thinking: Tools and Techniques to Help Students Question Their Assumptions. San Francisco, CA: Jossey-Bass, 2011.

Course Topics: 
1-Critique vs Criticism (2 lectures)
2-Traits of a Critical Thinker (2 lectures)
3- Inferences VS Assumption: Science vs Pseudoscience (2 lectures)
4- How Do We Know What We Know I: Reading and Discussion/Group Work (4 lectures)
5- Deductive and Inductive Reasoning: Reading and Class Work (2 lectures)
6- Evaluating Arguments I: Reading and Discussion/Group Work (4 lectures)
7-Major Fallacies: Reading Discussion/Group Work/ Class Activities (4 lectures)
8-The Scientific Method: Opportunities/Challenges/Limitations (4 lectures)
9- Triangulation and Alternative Perspectives (2 lectures)
10- Debating Contemporary Challenges I: Anthropocentrism, Ecocentrism and AI (4 lectures)

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 2 x 75-minute sessions per week. Instruction will be a combination of lectures/seminar discussions, in-class composing/writing, and small-group discussions/debating. Skills introduced in this Critical Thinking course will be extended and developed.
 
 
Assessment: 
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Individual Writing: Critical Reflection (in class)
 10%

 30%

 10%

Additional Grading Policy Information:
Plagiarism: 
 Students who are found to plagiarize (copy in any form including using generative AI) text, images, or other materials at any percentage of the total volume of an assignment will receive a warning and one opportunity to completely revise their work. The maximum students can receive for a revised assessment is 80%.

A second instance of plagiarism will result in an F on the assignment and a report to the Academic Integrity Committee. Students are advised to consult the Handbook for all policies related to plagiarism and other forms of cheating.

Grade Review Timeframe: 
After assessment marks are announced, students have one week to request further clarification of the grade awarded. After the one-week period, no further grade discussions will be considered.

All assignments are due on the date stated by the instructor on this syllabus. There are to be no late assignments. Late assignments will be penalized 10% per day (one full letter grade) until submitted.
No extensions of more than one week may be granted. 
Assignments submitted after the last day of the course will not be accepted unless announced otherwise. 

Extensions without penalties may be granted for health-related and other extenuating circumstances with appropriate documentation from the student and approved by Student Success Office.

HUMA 277 Introduction to Logical Reasoning 
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory - 3 credits) 

Prerequisite: ENGL 102 
Co-requisite: None

Course Catalog Description:
This course provides students with a solid introduction to logical thinking and critical analysis. Emphasis will be placed on arguments as basic units of thinking. By understanding the importance of the validity and soundness of reasoning, students will be able to identify, analyze, and evaluate arguments in scientific language and everyday discourse. 

Textbook: 
A Concise Introduction to Logic by P.J Hurley & Lori Watson, Publisher: Cengage Learning, 13th edition, 2018, ISBN: 9781305959767

Reference Materials: 
The Power of Logic by D. Howard-Snyder, F. Howard-Snyder, R. Wasserman, Publisher: McGraw-Hill, 5th edition, 2012, ISBN: 978-0078038198
An Introduction to Symbolic Logic by Susanne Katherina Langer, Publisher: Kessinger Publishing, 2009, ISBN: 9780486601649

Course Topics: 
Basic concepts: soundness, cogency, premises and conclusions (3 lectures)
Inference: deduction and induction (3 lectures) 
Language and meaning: intention and extension (3 lectures) 
Analogy: legal and moral reasoning (3 lectures) 
Categorical propositions: quality, quantity, and distribution (3 lectures) 
Immediate inference: traditional square of opposition and Venn diagrams (3 lectures)
Fallacies: formal and informal (3 lectures) 
Propositional logic: symbolic logic, symbols, and translation (3 lectures)
Truth functions and truth tables (3 lectures) 
Kinds of logical arguments (2 lectures) 
Natural deduction in propositional logic: rules of implications and replacement (6 lectures)
Theories of probability and probability calculus (3 lectures)
Statistical reasoning (3 lectures) 
The Scientific method of observation, experimentation, generalization, and Mill’s methods of causal inference (4 lectures) 

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 3 x 50 minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Course delivery focuses on lectures, problem solving, and in-class discussions. These discussions are related to informal logic and particularly fallacies, which occupy three sessions. The course is delivered through lectures, with an emphasis on problem solving and debating contemporary issues employing acquired logical skills. 

Assessment: 
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools. 
Coursework (Quizzes, assignments)
 30%

 10%

 25%

Instructor Policy on Late Submission of Assignments:
QUIZZES – There will be no make-up quizzes. Without an approved absence, the score is 0 (zero) for a missed quiz.
Semester Exam - There will be no make-up midterm. If you miss the midterm and you have an approved excuse, the final exam will carry all the weight of the combined midterm and the final exam (e.g. 50%). Note that this means missing the midterm without a valid excuse implies having a Zero.
If you are absent from the class or any of the quizzes/exams, note that it is the Registrar’s Office that will process and approve your absence excuse (doctor’s note, etc.), and then inform the faculty and instructors. Please do not expect that faculty will process your request.

MATH 242 Introduction to Probability and Statistics 
(3 Lecture – 3 Credits)

Prerequisite: MATH 112

Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces students to basic probability models and statistical methods for data analysis. The course will cover introductory probability theory, discrete and continuous probability distributions, elements of descriptive statistics, and different statistical inference methods such as estimation for the mean and the variance, hypothesis testing for the mean and the variance.

Textbook: 
Applied Statistics and Probability for Engineers by D. C. Montgomery and G.C. Runger, Publisher: Wiley, 7th Edition (online edition), 2018, ISBN: 978EEGRP45491.

Reference Materials: 
Probability and Statistics by M.H. De Groot and M.J. Schervish, Publisher: Pearson, 4th Edition (New International Edition), 2013, ISBN: 9781292025049. 
All of Statistics: A Concise Course in Statistical Inference, by L. Wasserman, Publisher: Springer, 2nd Corrected Edition, 2004, ISBN: 9780387402727.

Course Topics: 
Introduction to probability: sample spaces, axioms of probability and counting methods (4 lectures)
Conditional probability: law of total probability, Bayes’ theorem and independent events (3 lectures)
Discrete and continuous random variables: probability mass functions, probability density functions, cumulative distribution functions and their use in computing probabilities (4 lectures)
Two-dimensional random vectors, joint and marginal distributions (3 lectures)
Independent random variables and the notion of a random sample (1 lecture)
Expectation, variance and median of a random variable (3 lectures)
Covariance and correlation (2 lectures)
Common discrete probability distributions: Bernoulli, Binomial and Poisson (3 lectures)
Common continuous probability distributions: normal and gamma distributions (3 lectures)
Large sample theory: the law of large numbers, the central limit theorem and precision computations (4 lectures)
Elements of descriptive statistics: frequencies, tables and graphs (2 lectures)
Sampling distributions (I): sample mean, sample variance and their distributions when sampling from normal distributions (2 lectures)
Sampling distributions (II): the chi-squared, the t distribution (2 lectures)
Confidence intervals for the mean based on small normal samples or large arbitrary samples (3 lectures)
Confidence intervals for a proportion based on large arbitrary samples (2 lectures)
Hypotheses testing: t-tests of the mean, tests of a proportion, tests of the variance (4 lectures)

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures are either 3 x 50 minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Lectures are presented using the whiteboard, and may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides.

MATH 211 Differential Equations and Linear Algebra 
(4 Lecture, 0 Laboratory – 4 Credits)

Prerequisite: MATH 112
Co-requisite: None

Course Catalog Description: 
This course introduces ordinary differential equations with linear algebra. The course covers basic topics of linear algebra, including linear systems, basic properties of matrices, vector spaces, and eigenvalues and eigenvectors. The course also covers solution techniques for first-order differential equations, higher-order linear equations with constant coefficients, linear and almost linear systems of differential equations, and Laplace transforms.

Textbook:
Differential Equations and Linear Algebra by C. Edwards, D. Penney and David Calvis, Publisher: Pearson, 4th Global Edition, e-book, 2020, ISBN: 9780137313617.

Reference Materials:
Differential Equations and Linear Algebra by Gilbert Strang, Publisher: Wellesley-Cambridge, UK edition, 2015, ISBN: 9780980232790.

Course Topics:
Solving systems of linear algebraic equations (6 lectures)
Basic matrix properties, determinant, and inverse of a matrix (6 lectures)
Vector spaces and subspaces, row space, null space, and column space (6 lectures)
Basis, dimension, rank, and nullity (4 lectures)
Eigenvalues and eigenvectors (2 lectures)
Classification of differential equations (1 lecture)
First-order differential equations (8 lectures)
Second-order ordinary differential equations (8 lectures)
Applications of second-order ordinary differential equations (4 lectures)
Systems of linear differential equations (5 lectures)
Laplace transforms (5 lectures)
Solving initial value problems using Laplace transforms (5 lectures)

Structure and Learning Methodologies:
The weekly lectures amount to a total duration of 200 minutes and can be organized into either two or three sessions per week. In addition to the standard lecture presentations, supplementary materials such as handouts and PowerPoint slides may be provided.

Computer Resources: 
None.

All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.

Additional Information:
Assessment

Unreadable and/or poorly written algebraic steps in the solution process will be penalized.

If an answer is given but the working is not shown, then zero credit is awarded.

There are 4 quizzes scheduled in this course. Only the best 3 quiz scores will count towards your final grade, i.e. the lowest quiz score will be dropped. Each quiz will therefore count for 13.33% of your final grade for the course.

Calculator Policy

You may use a basic scientific calculator to help you with arithmetic computations in this course. The calculator must not have a graphing capability. The calculator must not be capable of performing symbolic manipulation. The calculator must not be capable of computing derivatives and integrals. A good example of an appropriate calculator is the Casio fx-82ES.

You will be permitted to use your (basic) calculator in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination and the Final Examination. Please do not try to use a more powerful calculator in the Quizzes and Examinations as it will be confiscated.

Mobile phones may not be used as calculators in the Quizzes, Midterm Examination or Final Examination. Mobile phones must be switched off during quizzes and exams and must be put away for the duration of the quiz/exam.

MATH 224 Real Analysis I
(4 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 4 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 101 and MATH 112
Course Catalog Description:
This course gives students a thorough understanding of essential concepts in analysis such as real numbers,
limits, continuity, and convergence of sequences and series. The course also covers a rigorous definition
of derivative and construction of the Riemann integral and their properties including the Fundamental
Theorem of Calculus. Students are required to read and write proofs using a precise knowledge of
definitions and theorems.
Textbook:
th
• Analysis with an Introduction to Proof by Steven R. Lay, Publisher: Pearson, 5 Edition, 2012,
ISBN: 9780321892539.
Reference Materials:
• A Problem Book in Real Analysis by A. G. Aksoy and M. A. Khamsi, Publisher: Springer Verlag,
2010, ISBN: 1441912959.
nd
• Undergraduate Analysis by Serge Lang, Publisher: Springer Verlag, 2 Edition, 2005, ISBN:
9788184896282.
nd
• Elementary Analysis by Kenneth A. Ross, Publisher: Springer, 2 Edition, 2013, ISBN:
978 1461462705.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction: review of tautologies, contradictions and equivalence, open sentences and quantifiers;
methods of proof in mathematics: direct method, contrapositive method, contradiction method (3
lectures)
2. Sets and functions: indexed families of sets and their properties, image and inverse image of functions
and their properties (5 lectures)
3. Real number systems: countability of R and Q, order properties of Q and its order incompleteness,
construction of R from Q using Dedekind cuts axioms (6 lectures)
4. Order completeness of R: least upper bound property and equivalent conditions including the nested
interval property, bounded sets, and their properties, supremum and infimum of sets, Bolzano-
Weierstrass theorem (7 lectures)
5. Topological properties of R: interior points, limit points, isolated points, boundary points, frontier
points, open sets, closed sets, perfect sets, compact sets, and connectedness (6 lectures)
6. Sequences: definition of convergent sequence, bounded sequences, monotone sequences and their
convergence, limsup and liminf and convergence criterion, subsequences, Cauchy sequences and their
convergence criterion (8 lectures)
7. Limits and continuity: epsilon-delta definition of limit, basic properties of continuous functions,
uniform continuity, bounded functions, continuous functions on compact sets, Intermediate Value
Theorem (10 lectures)
8. Differentiation: derivatives, the Mean Value theorem, L’Hospital’s Rule, Taylor’s theorem (5 lectures)
9. Riemann integral: definition, properties, the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (6 lectures)

--- Page 2 ---
MATH 224 Real Analysis I (4 – 0 – 4)
____________________________________________________________________________________
10. Series: series of numbers, definition of convergent series, geometric series, the comparison test,
integral test with proof, ratio test, absolute and conditional convergence, alternating series and Leibniz
test (4 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be held twice a week for one hour and 40 minutes each. Lectures will be presented using a
projected whiteboard, with the content from each lecture saved as a PDF file and uploaded to BlackBoard
for students.

real line; Archimedean principle and its application in computing the infimum of a set; natural,
integer, rational and irrational numbers; def. of real sequence; explicit and implicit forms of

MATH 242 Introduction to Probability and Statistics
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory, 0 Tutorials – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 112
Course Catalog Description:
This course introduces students to basic probability models and statistical methods for data analysis.
The course will cover introductory probability theory, discrete and continuous probability
distributions, elements of descriptive statistics, and different statistical inference methods such as
estimation for the mean and the variance, hypothesis testing for the mean and the variance.
Textbook:
Applied Statistics and Probability for Engineers by D. C. Montgomery and G.C. Runger, Publisher:
th
Wiley, 7 Edition (online edition), 2018, ISBN: 978EEGRP45491.
Reference Materials:
th
• Probability and Statistics by M.H. De Groot and M.J. Schervish, Publisher: Pearson, 4 Edition
(New International Edition), 2013. ISBN: 9781292025049.
• All of Statistics: A Concise Course in Statistical Inference, by L. Wasserman, Publisher:
nd
Springer, 2 Corrected Edition, 2004. ISBN: 9780387402727.
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to Probability: Sample spaces, axioms of probability and counting methods (4
lectures)
2. Conditional probability: Law of total probability, Bayes’ theorem and independent events (3
lectures)
3. Discrete and continuous random variables: probability mass functions, probability density
functions, cumulative distribution functions and their use in computing probabilities (4 lectures)
4. Two-dimensional random vectors, joint and marginal distributions (3 lectures)
5. Independent random variables and the notion of a random sample (1 lecture)
6. Expectation, variance and median of a random variable (3 lectures)
7. Covariance and correlation (2 lectures)
8. Common discrete probability distributions: Bernoulli, Binomial and Poisson (3 lectures)
9. Common continuous probability distributions: Normal and Gamma distributions (3 lectures)
10. Large Sample Theory: the law of large numbers, the central limit theorem and precision
computations (4 lectures)
11. Elements of descriptive statistics: frequencies, tables and graphs (2 lectures)
12. Sampling distributions (I): Sample Mean, Sample Variance and their distributions when
sampling from Normal distributions (2 lectures)
13. Sampling distributions (II): The chi-squared, the t distribution (2 lectures)
14. Confidence intervals for the mean based on small normal samples or large arbitrary samples (3
lectures)
15. Confidence intervals for a proportion based on large arbitrary samples (2 lectures)
16. Hypotheses testing: t-tests of the mean, tests of a proportion, tests of the variance (4 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be either 3 x 50 minutes or 2 x 75 minutes per week. Lectures will be presented using
the whiteboard, and may be complemented with handouts and/or PowerPoint slides.
UCC: March 2021 1

--- Page 1 ---
BUSS 322 Fundamentals of Innovation & Entrepreneurship
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: Junior Standing
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalogue Description:
This course introduces students to an innovative and entrepreneurial mindset, which will help them develop a
creative problem-solving approach to challenges. Students design an opportunity analysis and a personal
business plan, allowing them to find better solutions to challenges in their career and life.
Textbook:
Entrepreneurship and Small Business by Paul Burns, Publisher: Red Globe Press, 5th Edition, 2021, ISBN:
978-1352012491
Reference Materials:
1. Change by Design: How Design Thinking Transforms Organizations and Inspires Innovation by Tim
Brown, Publisher: HarperCollins, 1st Edition, 2009, ISBN: 9780061766084
2. Creative Confidence: Unleashing the Creative Potential Within Us All by Tom Kelley and David Kelley,
Publisher: Crown Business, 2013, ISBN: 9780385349369
Course Topics:
PHASE I: INTRODUCTION TO INNOVATION
Session 1A: The innovative mindset (1.5 lectures)
Session 1B: Where ideas come from (1.5 lectures)
Session 2A: Creative confidence (1.5 lectures)
Session 2B: The Design Thinking process (1.5 lectures) Session 3A: Need finding and empathy (1.5 lectures)
Session 3B: Generating insights and reframing (1.5 lectures) Session 4A: Idea generation (1.5 lectures)
Session 4B: Prototyping and experimentation (1.5 lectures)
Session 5A: Influencing and inspiring others (1.5 lectures)
Session 5B: Leading for creative confidence and going global (1.5 lectures)
Sessions 6A/B: Design Thinking in practice (3 lectures)
PHASE II: INTRODUCTION TO ENTREPRENEURSHIP
Session 7A: The entrepreneurial mindset (vision, mission, and strategy) (1.5 lectures)
Session 7B: How an entrepreneur identifies ideas and opportunities (1.5 lectures)
Session 8A: Lean startup methods I (1.5 lectures)
Session 8B: Lean startup methods II (1.5 lectures)
Session 9A: Go-to-market methods (1.5 lectures)
Session 9B: Opportunity analysis in practice (1.5 lectures)
Session 10A: Sources of capital for entrepreneurs (1.5 lectures)
Session 10B: How entrepreneurs assemble and motivate a Team (1.5 lectures)
Session 11A: Legal matters in start-ups (1.5 lectures)
Session 11B: Pitching and presenting as an entrepreneur (1.5 lectures)
Sessions 12A/B: Opportunity analysis project (3 lectures)
PHASE III: INTRODUCTION TO GROWTH & CHANGE
BUSS322 – Summer 2025 Melanie Bowman

--- Page 2 ---
Session 13A: Principles of change and growth (1.5 lectures)
Session 13B: Capturing cross-organizational opportunities (1.5 lectures)
Session 14A: Innovation, success, and failure in large organizations (1.5 lectures)
Session 14B: Reflection on course content (1.5 lectures)
Sessions 15A/B: Personal business plan (3 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 4 x 100 minutes per week.
Interactive and participatory learner-centered instructional methods will be utilized to explore content focused
themes. Each session will include a mix of the following components: mini-lectures, discussion, interactive
activities, and open Q&A.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes, homework) 20%
Project #1: Design Thinking in Practice 20%
Project #2: Opportunity Analysis 20%
Final Project: Personal Business Plan 40%

Course Learning Outcomes, BSLOs & GELOs:
No Course Learning Outcomes BSLO’s GELO’s
1 Recognize the difference between design thinking, innovation, and d 1A, 1E, 2A, 2E
entrepreneurship, and identify a shared vocabulary for the process
elements of each.
2 Demonstrate the importance of entrepreneurial ecosystems and c 1A, 2C
innovation clusters to healthy societies and economies.
3 Integrate the skills necessary for the formation of effective teams and c 3B, 3C
practices for team culture development that respect diversity
4 Employ the best practices in scaling, organizational innovation, and b 1A, 2C, 2D
change management for the benefit of organizations and society.
5 Apply design thinking, innovation, and entrepreneurship to their own b 3A, 3D, 3E
career development and paths

Course Catalog Description:
The course acquaints students with the geographical, cultural, demographical, economic and
social aspects of UAE society. It highlights the evolution of the country from a traditional to a
modern society. Topics such as UAE political system, family, population, migration, youth, and
the development of civil society are addressed.
Textbook:
Title Publisher Author ISBN Year

Pedagogy – Learning Process:
• The course will be delivered through the following methodologies: lectures, class
discussions, presentations, films, documentaries, photos, illustrations and maps, internet
resources, field trips.
• Presentations, when used, will only display illustrations and titles, as there should be no
summaries for the content.
• Due to the nature of this course, it is important to depend more on discussions within the
classroom. The instructor is therefore responsible for encouraging students’ participation.

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
If the student is absent for a Midterm Exam or the Final Exam, he/she must apply KU rules and regulations to obtain an approval of the excuse. 
If a valid excuse is provided for a missed Midterm Exam, either the percentage for that exam will be added to the percentage for the Final Exam or a make-up exam will be scheduled.
Quiz attendance must be in your own lecture section. You may not attend a quiz in another section. 
Unless a valid excuse is provided, a mark of zero will be assigned for missed quizzes. 
For each lab report, failure to submit it by deadline results in zero mark. There is no make-up for pre-lab quizzes. Cheating in a lab report results in zero.

Grading Scheme
KU Grading System – Policy ACA 3350
The official Khalifa University of Science and Technology grading system uses letter grades with pluses and minuses.  Passing grades range from A to D; F is a Fail grade. Each letter grade is assigned a grade point on a four-point scale as set out below:

Additional letter grades are used by the Registrar to denote special cases. These letter grades do not have corresponding grade points and are not used in calculating a student’s grade point average.

Letter Grade
 Description
 
W
 Withdrew between end of late registration and deadline for course withdrawal
 
WP
 Withdrew Passing after the deadline for course withdrawal through the last day of classes. A WP grade must be approved by the dean (or designee)
 
WA
 Administratively withdrawn due to absences

Instructor Policy on late submission of assignments:
No make-up class tests (i.e., Test 1-4 as stated above) will be given. If a student misses a class test, the weight of the missed class test will be transferred to the Final Exam. 
As per University policy, a documented justifiable missed Mid-Semester Exam will receive the same grade as attained on the Final Exam (if the absence on the Mid-Semester Exam is approved by the University). An unapproved absence for the Mid-Semester Exam is not considered a justification and will be graded zero.
A make-up Final Exam will only be given if approved by the Registrar's Office for documented justifiable reasons. An unapproved absence for the Final exam is not considered a justification and will be graded zero (i.e. no make-up Final exam will be given). 
 
Honor Code Academic Pledge:
"As a Khalifa University student, I will not lie, cheat, steal, or use any unfair means in academic work and will behave according to university rules and UAE societal norms and expectations."

 Review
 

Additional Information:
In-Class Tests:
There will be four in-class tests during the semester. All of the tests will be 40 minutes in length and will cover material you should have mastered since the previous test, but do require you to understand and make use of material taught since the beginning of the course.
Final Exam:
The final exam will cover material from the entire course. The date for the final examination is determined by the Registrar's Office. 
Calculator Policy:
Non-programmable calculators may be used during the in-class tests and exams. Use of any other electronic device, including programmable calculators, during exams will result in an automatic zero score on the exam. Students are not allowed to share calculators.

ENGL 102 Academic English II
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratory – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: ENGL 101
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course extends skills in evidence-based reading, writing, and digital composition introduced in ENGL
101. Major assignments include writing a data-based technical report that includes a digital presentation
and a group-based funding proposal based on a realistic Request for Funding Proposal (RFP) with an oral
presentation.
Textbook:
Writing Science: How to Write Papers That Get Cited and Proposals That Get Funded by Joshua Schimel,
st
Publisher: Oxford University Press, 1 edition, 2011, ISBN: 9780199 760244
Reference Materials:
1. IEEE Editorial Style Manual (available online: https://www.ieee.org/content/dam/ieee-
org/ieee/web/org/conferences/style_references_manual.pdf)
2. University of Pittsburgh Library System: Citation Styles (available online:
https://pitt.libguides.com/citationhelp)
Course Topics:
1. Review: Writing about numbers, abstracting/summarization, citing, referencing (2 lectures)
2. Writing to specifications (6 lectures)
3. Planning/Timeline Development (3 lectures)
4. Using Excel/Writing a budget narrative (6 lectures)
5. Close reading and interpretation of research reports (6 lectures)
6. Using databases and multiple online references to support arguments (4 lectures)
7. Composing in multiple genres for a variety of audiences (8 lectures)
8. Avoiding plagiarism through paraphrasing and citation (2 lectures)
9. Advanced digital composition, including video and audio formats (8 lectures)
Structure and Learning Methodologies:
Lectures will be 2 x 75-minute sessions per week. Instruction will be a combination of lectures/seminar
discussions, writing/composing workshops, and small-group activities. Skills introduced in ENGL 101
will be extended and developed.
Laboratory and/or Computer Resources: None. Students will use their own laptops.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Individual Writing: Technical report 40%
Digital presentation of technical report 20%
Group (</= 3 students) proposal in response to a Request for Funding Proposal (RFP) 30%
Oral presentation of group proposal 10%

Additional Information:
At the discretion of the instructor, students receiving a grade of less than 70% for an assessment may
have the opportunity to revise or retake the assessment one time within a specific timeline. Note: If a
student resubmits an assignment, the maximum grade the resubmitted assignment can be awarded
is 70%.
Students are expected to attend the full class on time and participate in activities in order to be considered
present.
Students absent from in-class/live online graded assignments, writings, or quizzes will receive a zero (0)
for the activity.
Students who are found to cheat or plagiarize (copy in any form) text, images, or other materials may
receive a zero or a grade reduction and a report to the Chair of English and the Dean of the College of
Arts and Sciences. Depending on the classification of the violation, the case may be forwarded to the
Academic Integrity Council for review. Therefore, students are advised to consult the Handbook for all
policies related to plagiarism and other forms of cheating, especially ACA 3500.

MATH 232 Engineering Mathematics
(3 Lectures, 0 Laboratories – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: MATH 112
Co-requisite: None
Course Catalog Description:
This course covers selected topics from mathematical analysis with engineering applications, including
complex numbers, partial derivatives, gradient vectors, multiple integrals, and Fourier series.
Textbook:
th
Calculus by James Stewart, Publisher: CENGAGE Learning, 8 Edition (Metric Version), 2016. ISBN:
9781305266728.
th
Advanced Engineering Mathematics by Erwin Kreyszig, Publisher: Wiley, 10 Edition, 2011. ISBN:
9780470458365.
Reference Materials: None
Course Topics:
1. Introduction to Complex Numbers [Stewart Appendix ]. (6 lectures)
a. Complex arithmetic, complex argument, and polar form.
b. Complex powers, roots, trigonometric and exponential functions.
2. Partial Derivatives [Stewart 14.1-14.7]. (12 lectures)
a. Functions of several variables.
b. Limits and continuity (examples only).
c. Partial derivatives.
d. Tangent Planes and Linear Approximation (examples only).
e. Chain Rule.
f. Gradient vector and directional derivatives.
g. Minimum and Maximum values.
3. Multiple Integrals [Stewart 15.1-15.9]. (12 lectures)
a. Double integrals over rectangles.
b. Iterated integrals.
c. Double integrals over general regions.
d. Double integrals in polar coordinates.
e. Applications of double integrals.
f. Surface area.
g. Triple integrals in cylindrical and spherical coordinates.
4. Fourier Analysis [Kreyszig 11.1-11.4, 11.7-11.9]. (15 lectures)
a. Introduction to Fourier Series.
b. Periodic functions, Fourier sine and cosine series.
c. Complex Fourier series, Parseval’s theorem.
d. Fourier integrals, Fourier cosine and sine transforms.
e. Complex Fourier transform, convolution.
Note: One lecture is a 50 minute class period.

TEACHING PLAN (Lecture):
Week 1 General introduction to chemical engineering thermodynamics. Define units of pressure,
Ch. 1 temperature, density, mass, and moles, SI and English system, and conversion of units.
Week 2 First Law of Thermodynamics: Introducing relevant concepts and terminology, e.g.
Ch. 2 A internal energy, potential energy, kinetic energy, specific volume, heat capacity, heat,
work, enthalpy, state and state functions. Quiz 1
Week 3 Energy balance for a closed system: Applications
Ch. 2 A
Week 4 Analysis of open systems: Mass and Energy Balances.
Ch. 2 B
Week 5 Open systems: Selected applications, e.g. turbines, nozzle, etc. Quiz 2
Ch. 2 B
Week 6 Volumetric properties of pure fluids: PVT behavior of pure substances, ideal gas and
Ch. 3A-B equations of state (EoS)
Week 7 Cubic equations of state, theorem of corresponding states, generalized correlations for
Ch. 3C, D gases, generalized correlations for liquids. Quiz 3
Week 8 Heat Effec10 3/10s: Sensible heat effects, latent heat of pure substances. Midterm
Ch. 4A, B Exam.
o o
Week 9 Standard heat of reaction (∆ H), temperature dependence of ∆ H, heat effects of
Ch. 4B, C industrial reactions.
Week 10 Second law of thermodynamics: Introduction, heat engines, Carnot theorem. Quiz 4
Ch. 5A
Week 11 Second law of thermodynamics: Entropy, entropy change of an ideal gas, mathematical
Ch. 5B, C statement of the second law, Entropy balance for open systems.
rd
Week 12 Second law of thermodynamics: Ideal work, lost work, 3 law of thermodynamics.
Ch. 5C
Week 13 Thermodynamic Properties of fluids: Property relations for homogenous phases. Quiz 5
Ch. 6A
Week 14 Thermodynamic Properties of fluids: Residual properties, two phase systems,
Ch. 6B,C generalized property correlations for gases
Week 15 Revision and final preparation
Any additional information: NA

CHEG 230 Chemical Engineering Thermodynamics I
(3 Lectures – 3 Credits)
Prerequisite: CHEG 205
Co-requisite: PHYS 122
Course Catalog Description:
Fundamentals of classical thermodynamics for application to chemical engineering processes and
systems. Application of first and second laws to the analysis of thermodynamic cycles and processes;
volumetric and thermodynamic properties of liquids and gases; heat effects.
Textbook:
Introduction to Chemical Engineering Thermodynamics, J. M. Smith, H. C. Van Ness, and M. M.
Abbott, 8th edition, McGraw-Hill, New York, 2018, ISBN: 978-1-259-92189-6.
Reference Materials:
No Reference Materials
Course Topics
1. Introduction to thermodynamics (5 lectures)
2. First law of thermodynamics (6 lectures)
3. PVT behavior of pure fluids (9 lectures)
4. Heat effects (6 lectures)
5. Second law of thermodynamics (6 lectures)
6. Applications of first and second law to flow processes (4 lectures)
7. Thermodynamic properties of fluids (9 lectures)
Course structure and Learning Methodologies:
The course involves lectures (either 2x75 or 3x50 minutes per week) supported with PowerPoint
presentations. In several instances, the students will solve in-class exercises during the lecture hours.
Moreover, students will be given homework assignments.
Assessment:
All course learning outcomes are assessed using the following assessment tools.
Coursework (Quizzes, homework, projects) 30%
Semester Examination 30%
Final Examination 40%
Contribution to Engineering Students Outcomes:
1 2 3 4 5 6 7
H - - - - - -
Contribution to Engineering Students Learning Outcomes:
No Course Learning Outcomes Student
Outcomes
1 Describe the scope and basic concepts of thermodynamics 1
2 Demonstrate the first and second law of thermodynamics to industrially important 1
processes
